this removes visible strokes, not the fixed mask rendering cost.

Composition samples the eight directions in one bounded loop. Its nearest,
non-mipmapped mask, depth and style reads use explicit level zero on both backends,
avoiding implicit derivatives and duplicated fade code on software GPU drivers.
A live uniform bypasses fade metadata reads and distance calculations when no
resolved strict style uses fading; toggling it does not rebuild shaders or passes.
Material coverage sampling retains its normal mip selection.

Model publication refreshes outline membership after the winning single-model
or multipart hierarchy is installed. Provisional meshes cannot enter the mask;
failed or cancelled successors leave the retained visual and its outline intact.

World rendering now uses the existing coordinator for editor and RTT views as
well as Play. Editor synchronization keeps world-matrix freezing but never
freezes the active-mesh queue, which conflicts with FrameGraph object
collection. RTT outputs own sampleable depth through the existing target
lifetime. Current equal-output off/consumer costs and ordinary editor activity
observations are retained in the qualification report; the historical shadow
optimization profile is not evidence of this scheduling change's cost.

In test builds only, `?test=1&renderDiagnostics=1` publishes a throttled read-only
`data-render-diagnostics` snapshot on the ready viewport after its own presented
frame. It includes requested settings, engine-confirmed API/adapter, actual
canvas/output dimensions and resource accounting for Computer Use evidence.
Normal sessions and timing fixtures do not incur this serialization. Whole-frame
WebGPU GPU timing remains unsupported here: Babylon 9.29's encoder timestamp
fallback returns zero without a measurement, so that value is not reported as
GPU cost or fed to dynamic resolution. WebGL timer-query support is unchanged.
## Transactional generated visuals

Model preparation retains the current editor or Play hierarchy until the next source generation, material bindings and animation groups are usable. Staged GLBs borrow the exact requested MaterialLibrary generation's readiness, including current authored texture parameter admission; resolving a working predecessor does not bypass a pending successor. Library successors compile for their current mesh users before replacing predecessor materials. Imported and assigned mesh variants finish native shader preparation before the model bundle publishes. Texture leases retain their admission deadline, while each native shader variant uses the existing shader stall deadline. Slot invalidation, placeholder disposal and scene disposal cancel the pending model owner; late completions cannot publish it. A failed structural editor replacement keeps the prior visual, and obsolete staged roots are disposed when their apply generation is superseded. Playback borrows animation groups from the visual bundle; bundle retirement removes its matching playback references. Authored material replacement can release the predecessor with `MaterialLibrary.releaseInstance(instanceKey, assetGuid)` after publication; omitting the GUID still releases every private material for a retired owner.

Construction materials of 2D Panel, 2DMaterial, 2DButton and pivot planes (editor and Play) are owned through `createOverlayUnlitMaterial` and a `VisualBundle`, so they retire with their mesh; compiled library materials are never owned.

Bitmap text preflight includes the still-live representation and text siblings already staged in the same actor replacement. Each complete replacement publishes before the predecessor is retired. Allocation-limit rejection keeps the previous text and is retried only after the authored request, installed assets or native texture limit changes.

### Visual ownership verification pickup

Local checks that cannot fit available memory are deferred at the user's request (2026-09-22). Keep `BL_TEST_PROFILE=shared`, the shared resource configuration and normal admission; run these batches sequentially when resources permit. No skipped check is a pass.

Source checkpoint: `64599304`, branch `agent/engine-visual-implementation-e` (also integrated into `agent/engine-visual-lifecycle-e`). The baseline at `ea3c9667` had 5 failures and 6 passes in `visual-lifecycle.test.ts` and `text2d-bitmap.test.ts`: model/text material growth from 2 to 202, same-length source aliasing and allocation defects. The same two files passed 18 cases at `e36439b1`. Later editor staging, multipart accounting, texture-animation fan-out coverage and construction-material ownership changes remain unverified. Earlier passes do not certify this later checkpoint.

The source checkpoint above is historical. Resume on the current combined `agent/engine-followup-integration` branch with these explicit scopes; split a batch further if necessary. The material admission/native preparation and cancellation cases below are authored but unexecuted:

```powershell
$env:BL_TEST_PROFILE = 'shared'
pnpm --silent agent:wait local --script test '--' packages/render/src/model-material-preparation.test.ts packages/render/src/material-library.test.ts
pnpm --silent agent:wait local --script test '--' packages/render/src/visual-lifecycle.test.ts packages/render/src/text2d-bitmap.test.ts
pnpm --silent agent:wait local --script test '--' packages/render/src/glb-anim.test.ts packages/render/src/model-preview.test.ts packages/render/src/scene-lighting.test.ts
pnpm --silent agent:wait local --script test '--' packages/render/src/text2d-mesh.test.ts packages/render/src/text3d-mesh.test.ts packages/render/src/overlay-texture-quad.test.ts
pnpm --silent agent:wait local --script test '--' packages/render/src/snapshot-apply.test.ts packages/render/src/editor-scene.test.ts packages/render/src/editor-scene-sync.chunks.test.ts
```

Also pending: admitted, scoped `@babylonslate/render` and editor typechecks and ESLint on the changed files (use the resource-admitted owner wrapper in the texture pickup section). No browser test covers visual generations. Physical A16 verification is waived. The Babylon patch must combine this AssetContainer observer fix with the physics adapter patch before final verification. Visual delivery is included in draft [PR #663](https://github.com/hideoutgames/BabylonSlate/pull/663); verification, CI and merge remain open.

Environment replacement keeps the prior usable cube while the successor lease prepares. Strict scene readiness includes that pending admission, and both publication and rejection invalidate the readiness cache. A failed successor returns readiness to the retained working environment.

Material generations retain their compile-time texture leases until preparation settles, including when initial component overrides replace a sampler. After publication, pruning follows attached texture blocks and pending replacements, so asynchronous native graph rebuilds cannot prematurely release an upload.

Bitmap allocation preflight and rasterization use the same canvas font, top baseline, and alignment before measuring. Baseline-dependent native glyph bounds therefore fit the admitted cells; genuinely changed dimensions are still rejected before pixel allocation.

Texture installation preserves the existing legacy UASTC descriptor normalization before creating immutable upload Blobs. Stored asset bytes remain unchanged; repeated bindings reuse the installed content without repeating conversion or hashing.
## Spline

Add **General > Spline** to an actor or Class/Prefab to author a component-local 3D path. Details shares Water River's Curvature, Path Point Count and XYZ controls; **Closed Loop** joins the last point to the first when there are at least three points. Paths keep 2–128 points. Curvature 0 uses straight segments and 1 uses a centripetal Catmull–Rom curve through the control points on all three axes.

In Scene Details, use the component card's **Edit In Viewport** action to target its handles; actor selection defaults to the first editable shape. In Class/Prefab, select the component. Drag a point in the camera-facing plane, drag a faint midpoint to insert a point, or double-click a point to remove it. Use the Details XYZ fields for exact positioning. The curve previews live; release commits one undoable property change in Scene and Class/Prefab documents. Component and ancestor transforms apply normally. The curve is an editor helper and does not render in Play/player.

Spline and Water River share the core sampling implementation, typed point rows and viewport handle lifecycle. Water keeps its existing X/Z curve with linearly interpolated elevation and width; river point drags remain on the local horizontal plane. Water-only width, depth, shading and buoyancy stay on water components.

## Water

Water assets share one definition between the renderer and game worker. Realistic and Stylized presets expose colors, opacity, reflections, roughness, waves, ripples, foam, subsurface, sparkles, and density. Colors are authored in sRGB. Ocean, Lake, River, Puddle, and Global Water Volume components own their footprint, depth, wave scale, and current. River control points include elevation and a per-point Width Scale, and the path curves through them; lakes and puddles use elliptical bounds.

`WaterBuoyancyComponent` applies lift and drag at four support points through the native physics backend. It preserves collision impulses and angular motion, so a falling rigid body can depress and tip a floating body. Zero Volume automatically provides displacement for twice the body's mass; an explicit Volume uses cubic metres and the asset's Density uses kg/m³. Width, Length, Height, and Offset describe the floating volume. Existing Rigid Body and collider settings continue to control mass and collision response.

Explicit Volume is multiplied by the component's absolute world scale on all three axes. At the default density, `0.002 m³` supports a `1 kg` body halfway submerged; increasing it to `0.004 m³` leaves a quarter submerged. A fully submerged hull supports at most `Density × scaled Volume` kilograms, so insufficient capacity sinks. Changes apply during Play. Details shows six decimal places and a fine scrub increment for small hulls; Reset returns to automatic (`0`). Lift and drag use implicit surface integration to prevent large displacement-to-mass ratios launching objects out of the water.

The four support forces are solved together against native collider inertia, including offset centres of mass. The optional physics-port `getBodyImpulseResponse` query predicts linear/angular velocity changes and returns the world centre of mass without applying an impulse. Havok supplies its native principal inertia; the software backend uses its unit-inertia model. Custom backends without the query use that same fallback. Native collision resolution still owns contact and load transfer after the water impulses.

Surface queries and buoyancy use the same analytic waves and simulation clock as rendering. Bodies within the Water Blend Distance answer with one blended surface ([Water blending](#water-blending)). Other overlapping surfaces select the highest water surface, or the explicitly selected water actor. Disabled water does not participate in queries or buoyancy. Puddle depth limits its buoyant volume.

Water time (Play and the exported player):

- One simulated water clock (`WaterClock`, owned by the runtime driver) advances by each tick's captured step (`dt` × time dilation) together with the World clock, so `slomo` and script time dilation slow waves, drift, buoyancy, Sample Water Surface and the rendered water alike; pause and `slomo 0` freeze them, and `step` advances one step.
- The physics step (and buoyancy) evaluates the water at the time before its own step (`stepTime`); script queries sample the time including the current tick (`time`). A run of equal steps is computed as `start + ticks × step`, so an undilated session matches the former `tickIndex × dt` clock exactly; under dilation a rate change no longer rescales the whole history (time used to jump).
- `stepMain` sends `waterTime { seconds, frameId }`: the step's water time with the snapshot frame that tick publishes. The renderer keeps the last 8 frames' times (`setSceneWaterTime(scene, seconds, frameId)`); snapshot apply pairs the water with the sampled frames (`sampleSceneWaterFrame`), and the water draws the time interpolated between the previous and next frame at the pose alpha. At a published frame the rendered water is the water physics evaluated; a frame whose time has not arrived yet keeps the newest earlier one.
- The clock is not saved or traced: Save Game restores selected actor state into the running session (like the World tick index, the clock keeps running), and trace replay re-runs ticks from the start, which reproduces the clock when the dilation is the same (dilation changes are not recorded in traces).
- The editor viewport and asset previews have no simulation: their water follows real time (`setSceneWaterTime` without a frame, or the engine frame delta) and holds while game time is paused.

Water velocity from a query (and so buoyancy) has two parts:

- X/Z: the current plus the waves' horizontal orbital (particle) velocity and a small mean-drift term (`waterSurfaceDrift`, faded with the offset near banks). That term cancels the backward average a fixed point under Gerstner waves would otherwise see, so a stationary support feels no net push. Buoyancy drag pulls each support toward this velocity, so floating objects sway with the swell.
- Floating objects rock in place; only the current carries them:
  - A hull that follows the orbit sees no backward average, so buoyancy re-weights the term per wave component by the share a support relaxing at the hull's drag rate still sees, ω²/(ω² + rate²) (`waterWaveDrift(..., couplingRate)`).
  - Horizontal drag uses each support's recent peak submersion: it rises at once and settles over 8 s. The instantaneous value correlates with the orbital velocity and pushed hulls metres per minute along or against the waves.
  - A small residual drift remains, depending on hull size and drag. On default waves the buoyancy tests' hull moves 0.16 m (drag 2.5) and 0.28 m (drag 20) in 50 s; with only the fixed-point term and instantaneous submersion it moved 3.4 m and 4.2 m.
- Y: the current's slope component plus the Eulerian rate of surface height at the queried X/Z, which drives the buoyancy spring/damper.

Worker caching:

- The game worker evaluates water for each physics step, always afresh, and separately for Sample Water Surface calls (`WaterWorld.query`), into a query state of their own so a query never replaces the poses or clock the step evaluated. Queries within a tick reuse the query state's evaluation; an unfiltered one also serves queries filtered to one water actor. Before reusing it, a query re-reads only what the evaluation depended on (water components' variables and transforms; while a surface is enabled, also removal and landscape components and the actors' transforms and parents; and the actor count), so a script that resizes or moves water and then queries in the same tick sees the change. A water component added to an existing actor mid-tick appears from the next tick. Both states reuse each component's normalized properties while its variables are unchanged.
- Component properties are re-normalized only when a variable value is replaced, so per-tick work is mostly transforms, and long-lived bodies keep their caches (for example a river's sampled centreline). A value mutated in place (for example a river point array edited without reassigning it) is not detected.
- The refresh builds an actor index only for attached (parented) water, so unparented water adds no per-tick map.
- A query outside a finite body's rest footprint returns before inverting the waves, so puddles and lakes far from a probe cost one footprint test, not a Gerstner inversion.


To start, create a **Water** asset and choose **Realistic** or **Stylized** (and, for Stylized, the **Painted** or **Toon** look). Open its Preview and Details to tune the look. Drag it from Place Actors into a 3D scene to create a lake, or add an Ocean, Lake, River, or Puddle component and select that asset. Components without an assigned asset use the Realistic defaults. A missing assigned asset also falls back to those defaults in rendering and simulation.

- Ocean is a finite rectangular volume. Width, Length and the actor/component scale resize its bounds; it stays in place when the camera moves. Queries outside those bounds return Found false. Existing Ocean components now use these finite bounds; use **Global Water Volume** for the previous unbounded behavior.
- Global Water Volume (`GlobalWaterVolumeComponent`) extends to the camera far plane and is unbounded for queries. Set its elevation, Depth, Water asset, waves and current in Details; Width and Length do not apply. Its dense inner grid is anchored to world-sized cells; smoothly graded outer cells cover the horizon. Under an orbiting camera the dense cells shift toward its target (by half the eye-to-target distance, up to most of their half width), so water near the eye and in focus both carry real wave geometry. Beyond them (about ±12 m around their centre at the default Wave Length and Surface Resolution) the geometry flattens: per-pixel normals still show the swell, but contact foam and waterlines there stay at rest height.
- Lake and Puddle use elliptical surfaces. Puddle defaults are shallow and nearly still; its finite depth limits the displaced volume.
- River uses component-local control points with elevation, a width and a directed current. **Curvature** (0-1) blends straight reaches into a centripetal Catmull-Rom spline through every point (default 1); elevation and width interpolate linearly between points so the water never overshoots uphill. Each point's **Width Scale** multiplies Width, so a river can narrow or widen along its course. Rendering, queries and buoyancy use the same sampled centreline (`waterRiverCentreline`). Flow Speed reverses with a negative value.
- Selecting a single unlocked water actor shows its outline and shape handles on the gizmo layer. Ocean, Lake and Puddle have red Width and blue Length handles (resized symmetrically about the origin) and a green Depth handle below the centre; Global Water Volume has only Depth. Rivers show white point handles (drag across the water plane; elevation is kept), orange width handles beside each point, and faint midpoint handles that insert a point when dragged. Double-click a point to remove it (a river keeps two). The water reshapes live while dragging and one undoable change is committed on release. Point elevations and exact values remain editable in Details.
- Transforms resize and position the water volume, including signed scales. Waves, ripples, foam width and current speed use world metres: moving or stretching a volume does not stretch or drag their pattern. Wave Direction is a world X/Z heading; waves displace vertically and, with Steepness, horizontally (see Wave model below). Vertical surfaces cannot be sampled as water height.
- Surface Resolution (8-128) controls target world-space detail, multiplied by the project's Water **Mesh Density** (0.5–1.5, see the Water tier table). Hard caps still apply after the multiplier (`WATER_GLOBAL_CELL_BUDGET` 192 cells per side for Global Water, `WATER_FINITE_CELL_BUDGET` 160 for finite volumes, `WATER_RIVER_ROW_BUDGET` 512 river rows and `WATER_RIVER_COLUMN_BUDGET` 64 columns). Ocean, Lake, Puddle and River geometry is anchored to the world: an evenly spaced grid (up to 161 by 161 vertices; rivers up to 65 across and roughly 530 rows along) depends only on the body, the volume's rotation and scale and the Mesh Density, never on the camera, so moving the view does not reshape the surface or flatten its waves. Wave components shorter than the grid spacing fade uniformly across the surface; per-pixel normals retain the detail. A lower density therefore flattens more of the shortest swell on screen, while floating objects keep bobbing on it. Only Global Water Volume follows the camera, with world-snapped inner cells and graded outer cells. Physics queries retain the full analytic waves at all distances.
- Each water material compiles only its own style: the plugin sets `SLATE_WATER_STYLIZED` from the asset's Style and, for Stylized, `SLATE_WATER_TOON` from its Stylized Look, so the shader-size budget is spent once. Editing Style rebuilds the water in the asset preview, the editor scene and Play; editing Stylized Look recompiles it in place.
- Live definition edits: `updateWaterMeshDefinition(mesh, definition)` applies an edited Water definition to a built surface in place. Built-in shading reads the definition on every bind (the swell constants, so Steepness, Wave Model, Peak Sharpness and Wave Seed; Color Variation; the Refraction amount; the FFT band's Detail Waves gain), so the call re-applies the material's Roughness and Reflection Strength and otherwise only invalidates what follows the asset:
  - Defines: `WaterMaterialPlugin.definitionChanged()` re-evaluates the features the asset runs (Refraction or Detail Waves crossing zero, Object Reflections) and marks the defines dirty once only when Wave Model, Stylized Look or a feature term crossing zero changes the asset's own defines; uniform-only edits cost no define work.
  - The surface's scene-copy intent moves to the new Refraction and Object Reflections, so admission and disposal stay counted.
  - Culling bounds re-pad for the new wave envelopes (Wave Height, Steepness, Detail Waves); the contact range of both fields refreshes (forcing the terrain field to re-measure its margin); CPU-displaced vertices (Custom Material) resample even while the clock is paused.
  - Wave Length changes the grid step, so the next placement builds a new rest grid in place (Global Water included) with its per-vertex rest data; no other definition field rebuilds the grid.
  - The scene's water clock keeps running. Style and Custom Material need a rebuild: the call returns false and changes nothing. Only the asset Preview, a finite Lake, uses it; the editor scene and Play still rebuild water when its asset changes.
- The Water asset **Preview** builds its scene and mesh once per shared engine, Style, Custom Material and revision of everything that material reaches: each Material, Material Instance parent and Material Function counts by its open document content while open (otherwise its registry entry), and each Texture by the saved header fields that decide its uploaded bytes (payload and chunks, without encode progress such as Compression State), because the preview loads the saved bytes. Editing any of them in another tab, saving a Texture, reimporting it or committing its encode still refreshes the preview; a queued, running or failed encode does not. Every other Details edit, including each scrub step, updates the existing mesh without restarting the wave clock; a Custom Material that finishes loading after later edits is built with the latest values. Project render settings (Water quality included, without Scene Linear effects, which the preview cannot show) apply to the existing preview scene without a rebuild.
- Shading tiers: the numeric define `SLATE_WATER_QUALITY` (0 Low, 1 Medium, 2 High, 3 Ultra; `WATER_SHADING_TIERS`) follows the device-clamped project **Water Shading Detail**. Generated code varies only through `#if SLATE_WATER_QUALITY >= n` (each directive on its own line), so Babylon's define-keyed effect cache stays correct on WebGL2 and WebGPU. See [Water shading per tier](#water-shading-per-tier).
  - `WaterMaterialPlugin.syncQuality(scene)` compares the cached `sceneWaterQualityDeviceClamp(scene)` object (one identity compare; it changes with the Water revision or the colour pipeline) every frame from the water update and before each readiness check; only a changed Shading Detail, or a change in the refraction and reflection features the asset runs, marks the defines dirty, and a frozen Play material also gets `markDirty(true)`, because frozen materials skip define preparation. Unbound or new materials declare High in the constructor and resolve the scene's tier at creation.
  - Feature defines from the asset, on every tier: `SLATE_WATER_SPARKLES`, `SLATE_WATER_CREST_FOAM`, `SLATE_WATER_SURFACE_FOAM` and `SLATE_WATER_SSS` (`WATER_FEATURE_DEFINES`) are set only when Sparkles, Crest Foam, Surface Foam or Subsurface is above zero, so a zero-valued feature costs nothing. Editing a value across zero recompiles that water.
  - Uniform-only terms moved to the CPU (`hardBindForSubMesh`): chop and capillary directions, wavenumbers and clock phases (`slateWaterChop0-5`, `slateWaterCapillary0-2`, phases reduced in float64), the swell's slope sums (`slateWaterSea`: Σk·a, the crest-fold denominator, and Low's skipped variance and slope), the contact ripples' wavenumber, phase and the wind heading (`slateWaterRipple`), the shore swash clock, cycle count, bore wavenumber and excursion (`slateWaterSwash`), the contact ripple bursts' delay (`slateWaterContactInfo.w`), and per-asset factors (`slateWaterTerms`: contact foam strength, Crest Foam on, the sea-state slope-variance cap and chop factor). `slateWaterSwellInfo.z` carries Color Variation.
  - Swell slots hold the components by descending slope (k·a), so Low's fragment evaluates the three that shape the light most; the vertex shader still displaces with all of them.
  - The terrain field's four shoreline taps run only inside the shoreline branch (known terrain with waves), with explicit level-0 sampling, so open water pays two texture fetches. Empty removal slots return before their matrix multiply.
- Refraction and object reflections (built-in water, `water-material.ts`). Views on the Forward FrameGraph split their object pass around a packed colour and linear-depth copy while copy-sampling water is visible (see Forward FrameGraph task order); flat bodies at Planar quality also get the view's planar reflection. Each feature compiles per render pass only where it can run (`WATER_OBJECT_DEFINES`):
  - `SLATE_WATER_REFRACTION`: asset Refraction above 0, device-effective Water Refraction, and a scene copy registered for the pass being drawn (`isMainWaterPass`). The view ray bends by the view-space surface tilt × Refraction × the water thickness behind the surface (capped at 3 m) ÷ view depth, driven by the swell and the first chop octaves only (`REFRACTION_OCTAVES`; the finer chop, capillaries and FFT band stay in the shading normal, so the bed no longer shimmers like jelly), with the shift capped in pixels of the copy (28 within 6 m of view depth, 3 beyond 60 m; orthographic views, `projection[3][3]` = 1, bend by the same view-space amount at every distance) and reads the copy (`slateWaterSceneSampler`: colour filtered, depth from the nearest texel so silhouettes do not halo). A bent ray that lands on something in front of the water keeps the straight ray, so nothing above the surface leaks into it. The copy's depth also bounds the bottom estimate (similar triangles along the view ray through the eye; along the camera's forward axis in orthographic views, whose rays are parallel), so absorption and the Shallow-to-Deep tint follow what actually lies below: the bed shows through shallows and deep water fades to its colour. Realistic adds (1 − F) × transmittance × (1 − foam) × shore fade of the refracted colour and keeps coverage only for the shore fade; Stylized lays its colour over the refracted scene by its own opacity. The refracted light (`swRefracted`, its share `swRefractedWeight`) joins the output in `CUSTOM_FRAGMENT_BEFORE_FOG`, apart from the water's own light. Under scene fog it is divided by the fog factor, less the fog colour at its share, so the water's fog applies only to its own light and the already fogged background stays as blending left it. In Scene Linear (`IMAGEPROCESSINGPOSTPROCESS`, an HDR target that never clips at 1) it stays out of the coverage estimate, so a background brighter than 1 cannot raise coverage and draw a dark contour along the shore fade. 8-bit display targets keep counting it (their background never exceeds 1). Where the copy texel holds something in front of the water (beside a foreground silhouette, at copy resolution) the pixel keeps the blended surface. Contact foam and the shore fade are unchanged.
  - `SLATE_WATER_SSR` with `SLATE_WATER_SSR_STEPS` (Reflection Steps): asset Object Reflections under Screen Space reflections, or Planar's Screen Space fallback, with a copy for the pass. The march runs from the eye-relative surface point along the reflected ray until the view's far plane (at most 500 units), the screen edge or just short of the eye; its steps are evenly spaced on screen with perspective-correct depth, and it is written per language (`toWgsl` has no loops).
    - A step hits when the ray lies behind the copy's depth there and crossed that depth during the step, so passing behind a foreground object is not a hit.
    - When a step only passes the depth the previous step saw while it sees something farther itself (the far side of a face shorter on screen than one step, such as the top of a wall or a box), one probe where the ray reached that depth catches it. Without it, the upper part of an object facing the water dropped out of its reflection.
    - Four bisection steps refine the hit. It stands only within an assumed object thickness (`SSR_THICKNESS`: 0.2 units plus 3% of the depth) of the surface its nearest copy texel holds, and takes that texel's colour, so a refinement that settles beside a silhouette misses and keeps the sky instead of reflecting the background there. A downsampled copy averages each texel's colour but keeps its nearest depth, so a silhouette texel there takes the colour of its neighbour away from the farther side (four depth loads and one texel load, only on a hit). The hit fades out over the outer half of that thickness, so a ray grazing an object's back never switches between the object and the sky as waves move it.
    - Hits fade over the outer 6% of the screen, the last 30% of the march and roughness 0.2–0.45; a hit whose scene point lies under the water plane is rejected, since the copy holds submerged geometry, and hits fade in over the first quarter metre above it, so a beach meeting the water reflects no stair-stepped edge of the copy's texels along its waterline. Misses keep the sky or environment reflection. Faces the view does not see (an object's underside) cannot be reflected; the planar mirror draws them. No per-pixel jitter: without a temporal resolve it would only add noise.
  - `SLATE_WATER_PLANAR`: Object Reflections at device-effective Planar on a flat body (`WaterMaterialPlugin.flat`, which the water mesh keeps current when a volume tilts). Each draw looks the reflection up with `waterPlanarReflectionForCamera`; only when it mirrors this mesh does `slateWaterPlanar.x` turn it on. The lookup follows the reflected ray: its point one eye distance beyond the fragment's eye-relative position (`PLANAR_REACH`) projects through `slateWaterPlanarMatrix` to uv = 0.5 + 0.5·c.xy/c.w. On flat water that ray continues the mirrored view ray, so the lookup is the fragment's own mirror point; on waves the reflection breaks up with the waves as the environment and the march do (a small fixed shift by the surface tilt used to leave wavy Ultra water mirror-flat, flatter than High's). Colour is divided by coverage and decoded when display-encoded, and coverage blends it over the sky. Other bodies at Planar march when the copy exists (the Screen Space fallback) and keep the sky otherwise.
  - Composition: object hits replace the sky or environment reflection with the water's Fresnel × Reflection Strength (`finalRadianceScaled` with an environment, emissive without one), before foam and the shore fade hide the mirror. Stylized replaces its painted sky reflection by its Fresnel weight, never over foam.
  - Bodies share their CPU constants. Definitions with the same wave fields (every body of one asset holds its own copy) share one interned wave set (`waterWaveSet`, also in the worker), and the swell kernel's per-frame constants (`waterWaveShaderConstants`, the warp, the crest count and slope sums) are computed once per wave set, Wave Scale, tier, clock and floating origin (`sharedSwell`), not per body and draw. A material packs its bound vec4s without allocation and reuses them for the next draw of the same render (the depth pre-pass and colour draws) while none of their inputs changed: the render, clock, origin, Wave Scale, tier, reflection source, wave set, blend partner and each definition field they read (`frameInputsChanged`), so an in-place asset edit still applies at the next draw. Effects are already shared by Babylon between materials with identical defines: 32 lakes compile one water effect per pass variant.
  - Binding is per draw and allocation-free: `hardBindForSubMesh` reads `waterSceneCopyForPass(scene, currentRenderPassId)` (`slateWaterScreen` = 1 / output size, Refraction, march distance; `invSize` is read every draw because an in-place backbuffer resize changes it) and the planar lookup, only for variants that compile those features; `bindForSubMesh` binds the copy and planar textures, or Babylon's empty texture in a frame without them. Defines change only when the device-effective quality, the colour pipeline, the asset or the body's flatness changes; copy presence is fixed per pass because the copy registers its pass before that pass's first readiness probe. Nothing is marked dirty per frame.
  - Samplers: Realistic water binds the environment cube, BRDF LUT, terrain field and contacts (4), plus the copy (5), the planar reflection (6) and the FFT detail band (7, see FFT detail in the shader below), within `MATERIAL_SAMPLER_RESERVE` (8).
  - Zero cost when unused: Low compiles neither copy feature nor planar (no copy is planned, sky only); Medium compiles refraction only; passes without a copy (Render Target Captures, the planar mirror) compile no copy feature, and at Planar they bind the empty texture behind a uniform branch.
- Project Scalability **Water** caps render-only cost (shading detail, mesh density, contact resolution, refraction, reflections, FFT detail); see the Water tier table under rendering scalability. Water rendering gates on the device-effective `sceneWaterQualityDeviceClamp(scene).quality` (and `screenSpaceFallback` for Planar on non-flat water) and compares `sceneWaterQualityRevision(scene)` on its next prepare; queries and buoyancy never read it.
- Realistic is lit PBR with an open-ocean art direction (Beer-Lambert water in the style of Unreal Water, Crest and Atlas, looking toward Uncharted 4 and Assassin's Creed Black Flag seas; unlit at Low Shading Detail, see [Water shading per tier](#water-shading-per-tier)). Two regimes come from physical inputs rather than separate looks, so they meet without seams: clear tropical shallows wherever the floor shows (water depth from the terrain field or the refraction copy), and a grey, whitecapped storm sea under a sky the sun does not outshine and a strong Crest Foam on a steep sea; elsewhere it keeps the open-ocean look:
  - Value structure: a dark navy body close up, brightening through Fresnel toward a soft horizon. Fresnel (F0 ≈ 0.02) is the facet's own Schlick term and replaces the environment BRDF's (`finalRadianceScaled` is rebuilt from the environment radiance), so the lifted reflection normal and the filtered roughness no longer dim grazing water. Facets turned nearly edge-on to the eye count as seen at no less than about half the view's own angle (masking), close water looked down into reflects up to 50% less (art direction: its body shows), and toward the horizon an aerial-perspective haze (`OCEAN_HAZE_PER_METRE`, gated to grazing views) takes the far sea to a pure mirror of the horizon sky, so it melts into the sky without a dark horizon line. In that haze the filtered blur relaxes (to a quarter) and the lifted reflection ray drops back to just above the horizon, so the farthest water mirrors the sky's own horizon band rather than a blurred average of the clouds higher up, and no seam separates it from the sky drawn above it. Far away a pixel's reflection and Fresnel follow the mean surface (half the swell, toward level, by the pixel footprint) and gain roughness, so the far sea is a soft gradient rather than ruler-straight bands and cloud detail melts into sky colour toward the horizon. Output is premultiplied by coverage (`CUSTOM_FRAGMENT_BEFORE_FOG` divides by alpha), so the reflection stays visible over clear shallows while the background shows through (1 − F) × transmittance. Bright glints raise coverage instead of clipping (refracted light excepted in Scene Linear, see refraction above).
  - Transmittance is Beer-Lambert absorption down to the floor and back along the refracted ray, through clear water: Depth Color Distance is how deep the floor stays visible, absorption runs over five times that path (the slowest channel), a little wavelength-neutral scattering (`TURBIDITY`) hands the bed over to the in-scattered body, and past about a third of Depth Color Distance the floor fades into the water's own colour (fully by about 0.85 of it), so a seabed that ends or drops off never shows its edge. Opacity caps the absorption path rather than each channel. From Medium it is per channel (`slateWaterAbsorb`, from Shallow Color, the colour shallows take, on the CPU in linear space: each channel's optical depth leaves it at its share of the brightest channel over a small common floor, `0.2 − ln(c / max)`, normalized to the slowest), so a cyan Shallow Color absorbs red many times faster than blue and green a few times faster, as clear sea water does: sand under shallows turns turquoise and azure, deeper water blue, and the in-scattered light fills only the channels the water absorbed (`swScatterT`). A grey Shallow Color absorbs evenly. Underwater daylight: with refraction, the refracted bed cools toward a neutral, slightly blue grey (`UNDERWATER_COOL` of the way within the first half metre) and darkens as wet sand does (`UNDERWATER_WET`), so sand under clear shallows reads cyan rather than green and shallows read as a bed under water rather than a glow around the shore; the refraction bend is 0.55 of Stylized's, so what lies under clear water wobbles with the waves rather than smearing sideways. Low cannot tint a blended background per channel: it passes the real bed at its fastest-fading channel's transmittance plus a fifth of the rest up to the mean (`LOW_BED_SHOWS`), and draws the remaining green and blue itself as a cooled wet-sand floor (`LOW_BED`) through the per-channel absorption, in wide soft patches, so its shallows take the same cyan as the per-channel tiers'. Where the scene copy holds no geometry behind the water (its sky depth), refracting water transmits nothing, so the sky colour behind open sea never shows through. From High, sun caustics shimmer on the floor: an iterated, self-warping trigonometric field (ALU only, after the widely used tileable water caustic) of thin bright filaments over slightly darker cells, about 4 m per tile and bent by a slow wide noise so the tile never repeats, evaluated where the refracted view ray meets the floor; they blur toward their mean with depth and before they would alias, fall off as 1 / (1 + depth), and need a clear sun some way up (none under an overcast sky). With refraction they modulate the refracted bed; without it they add a little light over a known terrain floor. At a terrain shoreline the thinnest water (under about 1.5 m) keeps the terrain field's smooth depth rather than the copy's nearest-texel bound, and the copy's in-front test blends over a small tolerance band, so the waterline never dithers into a checker; where the terrain field knows a bed under the water, the refracted path stands unless something rises well in front of the surface, so the copy's nearest texels never flip shallows between the refracted and blended paths from texel to texel. Toward a terrain field's outer edge its depth hands over smoothly to the open-water estimate, so no seam marks the field's edge.
  - In-scattering: the absorption tint sets transmittance, and the scattered body is Deep Color lit by the sky light and the sun entering the water on facets facing it (the swell and, more, the resolved chop, so even water seen straight down shows its ripples), so looking down into deep water shows Deep Color; colour appears where light passes through crests and over shallows (where the body leans a little toward a dimmer Shallow Color; clear water scatters little, so the shallows' colour is the bed's light through the absorption, never a glow of its own). Under an overcast or stormy sky (`slateWaterHorizon.w`: a hemispheric sky light the sun does not outshine, from the same ratio that fades the sun's glitter, `smoothstep(1.05, 1.5, sun / sky light)`) no sunlight reaches into the water to colour it: the body, the light through crests and churned water grey halfway toward a cool slate, so a storm sea takes the sky's colour. Troughs are darker and crests lighter (strongest at grazing views, fading out toward looking straight down and drifting with the large noise, so seen from above the swell's interference never prints as a lattice), and **Color Variation** drifts the body lighter and darker in wide patches (value, not hue). Sunlight entering the water weakens as the sun sinks (most of it reflects off at grazing incidence), so a sunset sea is mostly dark blue reflection under a warm glitter path.
  - **Subsurface** (0-2) drives the light through thin crest tops and chop tips: sunlight entering a crest scatters out toward an eye looking into the sun (a translucency lobe, `pow(saturate(dot(V, −normalize(L + 0.3 N))), 4)`, only on the upper part of a crest: its swell height above about 0.6 of the crest range, the Gerstner crest factor on Low too), in the sun's colour times what a crest transmits (per channel, by gentler ratios than a shallow's absorption, Beer-Lambert's −ln of the linear Shallow Color channel plus 0.6, at most five times the slowest, with blue absorbing at least as fast as green as it does through open water's plankton and dissolved matter; so a teal glow against the navy body even where the default azure Shallow Color tints clear shallows; skylight through crests takes Shallow Color with blue held to green too), strongest for a sun some way above the horizon and gone at sunset (a high sun lights crests from above rather than through them; a setting sun's light is dim and red, which the water absorbs). A little skylight glows through every such crest whatever the view, so crests separate from the troughs under an overcast sky too. The glow fades with the Fresnel (light the surface reflects never enters the crest), with distance (exp(−0.012 · metres)) and as crests stop being resolved, and is desaturated 40% toward a muted sea green, so open-ocean crests never glow like lagoon shallows. (A broad lobe without the crest gate used to tint a whole teal wedge of open sea around the sun's line, fixed to the view like a lens effect, and smear cyan blotches and stripes across deep water around islands.) Reflected rays stay above the horizon through a smooth maximum (no plateau of identical reflection directions on wave backs), and chop flattens into the swell toward the horizon, so distant water keeps reflecting sky rather than turning black.
  - Sky without an environment or skybox (no `REFLECTION` define): an analytic sky from the scene instead of a dim ambient term. `slateWaterSky` is the zenith: the background colour (what such a scene shows as its sky, linearized) deepened and saturated a little, or mostly (92%) the hemispheric sky light's colour where the scene has one, since that light is a better guide to the sky drawn behind the water (a dome, a grey storm) than the clear colour (the reflected sky takes about half of the light, which also carries bounce and fill: 0.45 overhead, 0.55 at the horizon, so a grey storm's far sea stays as grey as its sky); `slateWaterHorizon` is the background (shared, like the zenith, with a hemispheric light's sky colour), or the fog colour with fog on, converted to linear space exactly as Babylon's PBR fog converts it, so the far sea fades into its own fog without a brighter band before it. A low sun warms the whole horizon and, much more, the sky around its own azimuth, in the sun's colour deepened (squared) as a sunset horizon is more saturated than the light itself, and toward the sun's azimuth that glow replaces most of the cool sky light, so grazing water at sunset takes the glowing horizon's colour; an aureole surrounds the sun; rough water mirrors a blurred version. Fresnel and **Reflection Strength** scale it like the environment reflection, and reflected objects replace it where found. A found object is one sharp sample of what a rough, wavy pixel mirrors, so its coverage softens: facets whose mirror ray pointed below the horizon (lifted to it, see above) keep the sky instead of pasting whatever stands on the horizon across wave backs as flat patches, facets tilted far from the swell's own mirror direction fade, filtered roughness blends the reflection toward the sky, where neighbouring pixels' rays fan out 12 to 48 times wider than their view rays (steep swell some way off) the sky or environment shows instead, and the hit mask's one-pixel edges are feathered. Realistic finds objects along the swell's mirror ray (with a quarter of the facet's own), so what it reflects is one wobbling mirror image that the chop's facets break up through that coverage; per-facet rays hit unrelated texels of the copy from pixel to pixel and drew an island's beach as scattered, stair-stepped pale blocks on the water around it. With an environment or skybox, Babylon's reflection is unchanged. Wide wind slicks and rougher patches (the shared wide noises) vary the filtered roughness, so the far sea is neither one mirror nor one even sheen.
  - Up to six sharp-crested wind-chop octaves plus four capillary octaves down to about a fifteenth of the chop's base wavelength (Ripple Scale and Ripple Strength; counts per tier; the finest resolves only within a few metres of the camera) ride on the analytic swell, calm near the shore, and advect with the current. Rougher seas carry steeper chop. Each octave fades by the view ray's footprint on the rest plane, along its own direction from High up, which stays smooth across mesh triangles. Each octave's strength also rides an oblique envelope (`chopCrests`: 1.22 rad off its heading, alternating sides, at 0.7 of its wavenumber, between 0.55 and 1.45 with mean 1), so its crests are one or two wavelengths long instead of running in even parallel rows (corduroy). The envelopes travel forward through the chop on two clocks reduced on the CPU (`slateWaterChopShift.z` and `.w`: a at 1 / (3 + φ) ≈ 0.22 of the first octave's angular frequency, so the first octave's crests stand about 0.4 of a wavelength off when its envelope comes round again, b at √2 times a; octave i's envelope phase is a whole combination m·a + n·b, about a quarter of its own angular frequency), so crests grow and fade as they travel and after one period the chop's crests stand elsewhere. The clocks' irrational ratio means no two octaves' envelopes share a period and the envelopes never come back into step together (they used to run on one clock at whole multiples, so every envelope returned at once every 6.3 s at the defaults, with the first octave's crests nearly back in place too, a recurrence measured in Painted's still top view); the medium noise bends its phase 1.6 times as hard and the paired strengths drift over patches a few metres wide. Stylized chop rides the same envelope. Capillaries ride a noise-bent domain and gather in drifting patches, so close water breaks reflections into fine wind ripples rather than smooth chrome shapes. An octave a tier drops adds its slope variance to the filtered roughness, so a lower tier looks like filtered higher-tier water rather than glassier water. An octave whose phase advances more than about 0.6 to 1.3 radians per frame at 30 frames per second (`TEMPORAL_FADE`: its deep-water angular frequency times its speed and Wave Speed; at the defaults only the two finest capillaries) fades and adds its variance the same way, so ripples too fast for the frame rate never strobe the sun's glints from frame to frame.
  - The sun: Babylon's GGX lobe carries the broad sun path once, widened by the slope variance lost to filtering (detail at 0.85 of its variance) plus a floor for wind ripples below the smallest capillary octave (`OCEAN_SUB_CAPILLARY_VARIANCE`, scaled by the local chop gain, so Ripple Strength 0 keeps a mirror), all capped by sea state (0.004 for calm water up to about 0.02, Cox-Munk-like, for steep seas). The same roughness blurs the environment reflection, so a wind-roughened sea never mirrors cloud edges as hard blotches and the blur grows with distance. Sea slopes fall off like a Gaussian rather than GGX's long tail, so the sun's specular and glints fade by the facet tilt from the swell that the glint needs (`swSunReach`): a sun behind or high above the eye never lights steep chop crests as rows of white scratches. Where the lobe is bright it is log-compressed (more with distance), so its core keeps a gradient instead of flattening into a slab with hard edges. Specular antialiasing (`PIXEL_SLOPE_FILTER`, Toksvig- and Kaplanyan-style): the shading normal's own variation across the pixel (its slope's screen derivatives under a Gaussian pixel filter of 0.15 px², capped at 0.012 so a low resolution never smears the sun path into a slab) adds to that variance on every tier, outside the sea-state cap, so resolved detail the pixel cannot sample densely enough widens the lobe and the reflection's blur instead of sparkling into salt or crawling between frames. From Medium up, close water dims the broad lobe and draws facet glints instead: a lobe about three degrees wide around the sun's mirror direction on the resolved facets themselves (chop and capillaries), so close glints are the sun's mirror image on actual ripple faces. The facet normal's variation across the pixel widens that lobe, so a glint narrower than a pixel spreads over one instead of flickering, and lowers its peak by the square of that factor: a facet the pixel does not resolve hands its light to the broad lobe (which that variance widens too) instead of spreading a mirror's brightness into a white slab. Glints also fade as the facets stop being resolved. The capillaries no tier resolves break each facet glint into the same glitter (below, at 96% weight close by), so sun-facing chop faces sparkle with small, stable, surface-riding glints instead of shining as smooth chrome or mercury blobs. Farther out, where no facet is resolved, glitter (`swGlitter`, every tier, ALU only) gathers the lobe's light into glints over a dimmer path: the field averages 1, `GLITTER_BASE` of the lobe stays smooth under the glints and a glint concentrates at most `GLITTER_MAX` times the mean. There is one glint per cell of a world grid in rest (Lagrangian) space, so glints ride the waves instead of sliding over them; cells are at least `GLITTER_CELL_PX` (3) pixels across in their most compressed direction (two nested grid levels crossfaded by the footprint, each seeded by its absolute level, so nothing pops as the camera moves), so perspective draws far glints out into horizontal dashes. The lattice is bent by a smooth, bounded warp (0.45 cells, its Jacobian carried into the pixel footprint) and each glint sits anywhere within 40% of its cell, so glints never line up into rails as the view moves along a grid axis; glint sizes vary from 0.6 to 1.4 times, so near water never reads as confetti of equal dots. Far away only 18% of the lobe stays smooth (`GLITTER_BASE`; at 40% the far path read as a milky sheet with a few dashes), so the sun's path is a road of bright dashes over darker water, and the glitter weighs in from a dimmer part of the lobe (luminance 0.05-0.4), so a sunset's path breaks into glints too. Each glint is a small peaked spot seen through a Gaussian pixel filter (its covariance gains the filter's image under the footprint, keeping its energy, so a glint thinner than a pixel spreads and dims instead of jumping between pixels), living for `GLITTER_SHARE` (40%) of a slow 1.4 to 4 s cycle, over which it grows from a point to its full size and shrinks back (its size rather than only its brightness, so even a glint bright enough to saturate appears and leaves gradually), so the path shimmers instead of flickering. From Medium up glitter weighs in from pixels of about 2.5 cm (the facet glints carry the sun closer) and fades when the sun does not stand out against the sky light (overcast and storm skies, `slateWaterSky.w`: the sun's intensity over the hemispheric sky light's luminance where the scene has one, which a high sun does not inflate, otherwise over the ambient's, from 1.05 to 1.5). Glints are slightly warm and capped before the premultiplied coverage. The earlier field (one-pixel cells switching on and off at random, with no filtering) changed almost completely every frame. Low is unlit and evaluates one analytic lobe instead: the half vector's slope away from a half-flattened swell normal, spread about 1.6 times more along the view than across it (its variance includes the pixel's), so the path stretches toward the eye like a real sun path, log-compressed harder, and gathered into the same glitter (no texture), so it glitters instead of reading as one pale slab, dimmed a little close by so the path narrows toward the eye; out to the middle distance its glints gather on the facets Low resolves (a narrow lobe on its wave normal) and fade elsewhere, so they follow the waves rather than sprinkling evenly over the path; under an overcast or stormy sky it stays a soft sheen.
  - Foam is lit and matte: it becomes PBR albedo at full roughness, hides the mirror reflection, and receives sun, sky and shadows. From Medium a bubble web (the distance to the nearest of jittered Worley points, 3 × 3 neighbourhood with feature points anywhere in their cell, so no square lattice shows: 0 at the points, rising to 1 between them, bent by the blob and clump noises) with clumpy noise, drawn out along the wind (twice as long downwind as across, and about four times with Crest Foam well above its default, so storm foam streaks), is thresholded by one foam density (caps, swash, surf, whitecap residue and wind streaks alike), so dense foam is solid but keeps a few small round holes, and as it decays its holes grow at their own sizes until strands are left, then wind-drawn scraps and fragments. The pattern is the larger of the hole measure (90%) and the web's borders, so dense foam opens holes while thinning foam keeps only thin strands of lace along the borders (holes alone left thick, flat white worms; borders alone, a network of cracks), and no density leaves no trace of the web: open water and decaying residue never carry a faint translucent net. Medium draws its one web at about twice the large scale, so close foam shows lace; from High the large web churns (two re-seeded phases crossfade, so foam evolves rather than slides) and a second web about a third its size layers small bubbles into it. Before a web's cells shrink to a few pixels it gives way to a mottle of the blob noise, and dense cap cores keep the pattern's holes and some translucency (at most 0.65 coverage on their own), so mid-distance foam is never flat polygons or paint. Foam is a neutral white scatterer (Foam Color at 0.82-1 by thickness, and 0.72-1 by its own pattern, as bubbles shade one another, so it keeps an inner texture under any light instead of reading as flat paint) with a little sky fill, so it stays the brightest thing on the sea even under a dull sky; thick fresh foam is slightly brighter than its fraying lace, and thin foam reads thinner through lower coverage (sparser, softer and more translucent) rather than through a grey colour. Its slope warp is bounded, so storm slopes never shred it. Where the pattern is too fine to resolve, foam keeps the average coverage its density gives. Churned air lightens the water around foam.
  - Shores (the shared swash, see Shore swash below): the wash band at the waterline widens as each bore runs up and narrows as it drains. Over known terrain the bore's front carries dense foam up the beach, its furthest reach leaves a stranded line that fades as the water drains, and the backwash uncovers the shallows: there the water (its body, transmittance and refraction) gives way to a wet film that darkens the sand under a faint sheen of sky and sun and dries over about a cycle, so the wet band lags the swash and only the larger waves wet the top of the beach. Bores roll in through the surf (from Medium), each leaving thinner foam behind its front. Where the water is shallow for its waves (Wave Height over the rest depth above about 0.35, within about ten Foam Widths of a shore and only within about half the shore range of one, so the shelving-depth estimate of open or unknown water never breaks) crests break into surf that leaves thinner, decaying foam around them. Over known terrain the thinnest water (under about 0.3–0.5 m, deeper as a bore runs up, and at least a few pixels wide by the depth's screen derivative) darkens what lies under it with a soft edge, as a water film darkens sand, so the waterline reads as a wet band rather than a dark outline; the water shader does it alone (coverage on the blended path, a darker refracted bed with the copy), and terrain materials are untouched.
  - Whitecaps form on high, sharp swell crests (Crest Foam sets coverage, more steeply than before: the default 0.35 caps only the steepest crests, as lace whose dense cores need Crest Foam from about 0.35 to 0.8, so a calm preset never slides one long flat-white streak along a crest; below storm strength, see below, the wind chop's own crests never break, so open water carries no even dashes) once the sea is steep enough (the swell's total slope above about 0.25, fully from about 0.55): a gentle sea never breaks, so its open water carries no stray foam blobs, while a storm caps most of its crests. Crest sharpness, the Gerstner Jacobian, crest height and slowly drifting breaking zones drive them, so only some crests break: fresh cores are dense and bright, and a thinner skirt of decaying foam surrounds them. Their residue on the crests' back faces, in the same breaking zones, is drawn out downwind into streaks about five times longer than wide that drift with the wind and fade behind the crest (Low draws the same downwind residue from one stretched noise). Long gentle swell and small lake waves stay clear. Low's caps are a little rarer and have no dense cores: their density follows its streaky foam texture, so they open into streaks and holes rather than solid blobs. Storm breaking: with Crest Foam well above its default (from about 0.5, fully at 0.95) on a sea steep enough to break, the wind chop breaks as well as the swell's high crests: steep crests and chop crests in the drifting breaking zones whiten, driven by the swell's own height and never in its troughs (the windward backs only carry the trails), so whitecaps scatter over the whole sea as broken patches the web frays into lace and leave soft streaks downwind, with dark water between them, while the swell-crest caps thin so whole crests never whiten as unbroken bands (Low's storm caps form a little later and stay smaller). Aerial perspective fades distant foam with the water (to 30% at full haze), so far breakers never pile into a bright band along the horizon. Crest Foam 0 compiles caps and trails out.
  - **Surface Foam** (0-1) sets the strength and width of wind streaks: on a sea steep enough to break (the sea state above about 0.45, fully from about 0.8), the foam its breakers leave is drawn out along the wind into long, meandering streaks metres apart. They are contour lines of a value noise stretched about eleven times along the wind and bent by the wide noises, so their spacing, width and course vary and they never form a regular set of parallel lines; they drift downwind with the foam, are soft bands a few tens of centimetres wide, break into runs, join the foam density so the bubble web frays them into lace (Low, which has no web, breaks them into translucent scraps a few metres long along its streaky foam texture and its round clumps), and fade out with distance before they would alias. They used to be thin, static lines (strongest on Low) that ran straight across the sea and, far away, kept a faint average that perspective drew into streaks converging on the horizon. A gentle sea carries none, a fresh breeze a few faint ones and a storm many. They are ALU only, so Low draws them too. Churned air under breaking foam and surf (not under streaks) tints the water milky turquoise.
  - Known limits: refraction and object reflections need a view on the Forward FrameGraph (classic frames, Render Target Captures, the Water Preview and thumbnails keep the blended surface and the sky); the copy is taken after opaques, so water behind water and transparent objects or particles behind refracting water are not seen through it; the screen-space march reflects only what is on screen (the planar mirror covers off-screen objects for one dominant flat body per view); back faces shade as if seen from above, so there is no underwater view.
- Public water exports of `@babylonslate/render`: `setWaterGpuWaves`; `waterSceneCopyForPass` / `WaterSceneCopy`; `waterPlanarReflectionForCamera` / `waterPlanarReflectionDiagnostics`; `waterFftForSurface` / `waterFftDiagnostics`; the FFT CPU reference `waterFftLayout`, `waterFftInitialSpectrum`, `waterFftCycle` and `waterFftSynthesize`; and `clampWaterQualityToDevice` with its `WaterDeviceView` (`{ sceneLinear }`). The scene copy's admission and registry helpers, the planar owner's retention, the FFT simulation scheduler and its GPU pass builders stay internal to the package.
- Stylized water has two looks, chosen by the asset's **Stylized Look** (`stylizedLook`: `painted`, the default, or `toon`). Both are unlit but lit by the scene's light estimate (the strongest directional light `slateWaterSun` and hemispheric ambient `slateWaterLight`), ignore scene light slots (no PBR light loop), reflect objects only with Object Reflections on (off by default), and add no sampler; Low is ALU only.
  - Each material compiles one look: within `SLATE_WATER_STYLIZED`, the boolean `SLATE_WATER_TOON` (declared off in the plugin constructor, set when Style is Stylized and the look is Toon) selects `toonSource`, otherwise `paintedSource`. Both share the swell, chop, contact and depth code (`surfaceSource`; Painted also gathers the primary swell and bends its chop domain finer) and every uniform and binding, so the look costs no CPU work. Realistic ignores the look and keeps it stored. Realistic's sky-light horizon, glitter threshold and clear-water absorption do not apply to Stylized: it binds the fog colour or plain background as `slateWaterHorizon`, the sun's glitter against the ambient light (`slateWaterSky.w`), and an absorption (`slateWaterAbsorb`) whose channels within 15% of the brightest absorb alike.
  - Switching the look in Details keeps colours and wave settings, like Style; unlike Style it recompiles the built surface in place (`updateWaterMeshDefinition` → `definitionChanged`), so the asset Preview keeps its wave clock. The New Asset dialog's **Stylized Look** toggle (shown with Stylized) seeds that look's defaults. Assets saved without the field load as Painted with their stored values.
- **Painted** (`paintedSource`), in the spirit of Sea of Thieves: the swell shapes big readable waves in four soft tones, a Fresnel reflection of a soft painted sky on the swell's own normal, a luminous emerald glow under crests, a sun path of crisp glints, clear turquoise shallows and soft lace foam. Every tier is ALU only apart from the copy and reflection features:
  - Light: the sun and sky light's brightness lights the water and their hue only tints it (more of the sun's), with a floor that keeps the colours readable lit only by an environment. A low sun's light mostly glances off, so less of it lights the body; a warm low sun gilds the mirrored horizon, brightest and most golden where the waves' reflected rays point toward its azimuth and darker, partly gilded farther from it, so the far sea carries a golden path that breaks with the waves (the gilding used to saturate at every azimuth: one flat, uniform gold band); a dim sun against a bright sky light (overcast, storm) greys the sky and flattens and softens the mirror.
  - Colour: open water is Deep Color looking down, lighter and bluer toward grazing views, with **Color Variation** drifting wide areas greener or darker. The bed's depth sets the shallow-to-deep mix (refraction only bounds what shows through), and **Color Bands** above 1 quantise it with crisp edges (the Painted default is 0, a smooth gradient). A clear turquoise lagoon hugs every shore several metres out, however steeply the bed drops.
  - Waves: four broad tones by the primary swell's height (the components near Wave Length), with soft edges that widen by the pixel's change of height: ultramarine troughs, deep cyan flanks, bright cyan-teal backs and pale turquoise tops, saturated by a final chroma push and yellow-green on the tops where the eye looks toward the sun (Medium up). Toward the horizon the height contrast eases in patches of the wide noises (to 30% at most), so far crest rows fade in and out instead of banding the whole sea. Seen steeply from above the shading uses a phase-bent copy of the swell whose components drift in strength across wide patches, so the few components never print a dimple lattice. Close up the chop shades the water by a rounded copy of its octaves (`swDetailSoft`, a sine profile at `PAINTED_LUMP_SLOPE`, the finer octaves weighted less), so near water is broad soft lumps, not ridges, and the light falls on them in three soft steps (`swLitSteps`) whose edges widen with the pixel's change of facing, so no single ripple draws a step. Ripples are sparse brush strokes: two crossing families at `PAINTED_STROKE_K` of the first two capillary octaves' wavenumbers, running within about fifteen degrees of the wind and sliding slowly across it, shown only where a drifting noise gate stretched along the wind (`swStrokeN`; patches about a metre long and a third as wide) is high, the two families taking opposite halves of the gate so they never overlap into a lattice. They tilt the shading a little and draw thin bright cyan lines (`swStroke`) on the lit faces. Low gates its two triangle-wave families (and the crossing wavelet) by the medium and large noises it already evaluates, with no steps or yellow tops. Continuous ridge families used to stack into a sinewy grain (crumpled foil) at grazing angles. The Stylized chop octaves drift their phase (half as much on Low) and, in opposition between neighbours, their strength across the shared noises, and the chop domain bends at a finer scale from Medium, so close water never forms ribs; Painted bends the phase twice as hard across the medium noise and fades the pairs across it too, so its chop is short-crested (crests a wavelength or two long) instead of running in even rows across the foreground.
  - Sun, foam and caustics: the sun path tapers into a column toward the sun (`swCol`, wide near the eye and a few degrees across far away, a brighter core from Medium) with larger, sparser marks near the eye, and star sparkles are rarer and only inside the column. Foam holes are fewer, larger and rounder, lit foam leans cream and shaded foam cool blue-grey, and a thin darker wet edge rims the lace (Medium up); shore bands bulge in scallops along the shore and long thin lines trail seaward from the surf (`swSurfLines`, Medium up, only near a shore). Caustics are bolder, carried by the primary swell's slope so they breathe with the waves, and fade out by about 2.6 m of depth.
  - **Subsurface** (0-2): sunlight through the thin water on each crest face rising away from the eye glows emerald (Shallow Color leaning green), only at grazing views, strongest looking toward the sun and weaker from other sides with the sky's light; a warm low sun glows less and its glow shifts toward green-gold (a teal glow is off-key under a sunset).
  - Sky: an analytic sky from the scene's background or fog colour (deeper overhead, brighter at the horizon, tinted by the sky light), or the sky light's grey under an overcast one. Where the view has a scene copy (Medium up) its real sky colours replace half of it: three fixed copy taps just above the horizon and near the top of the view (seven from High), so the reflection is a smooth gradient of the actual sky; taps behind objects drop out. The reflection uses the swell's normal (a little chop and the ripples close up), keeps reflected rays just above the horizon, leans toward the water's own hue (more on Low) and stays below a mirror's strength (Fresnel × **Reflection Strength**, capped at 0.5 near the eye rising to 0.75 far away, and about 0.18 higher under a warm low sun, so a sunset sea mirrors its gilded sky instead of turning grey-taupe), so faces turned to the eye show the body and crests and wave backs mirror the sky. Far away the body takes the horizon's hue at its own brightness. Far away (and from above) the swell is shaded and mirrored through the phase-bent copy too, so the far field never reads as one wave train's even rows running to the horizon.
  - Sun: a glossy sheen on the swell, a path of crisp marks, a broad halo on far water and, from Medium with **Sparkles** above 0, star sparkles with arms only where a cell spans enough pixels (their lobe widened by its normal's variation across the pixel, keeping its energy). **Roughness** widens the path. The marks (`swMark`) are streaks drawn out across the view on a grid anchored to the water's rest surface, so they ride the waves instead of sliding over them (only a streak's orientation follows the eye's direction, which changes slowly). The grid's scale steps with the pixel footprint in octaves (two neighbouring octaves crossfaded, each seeded by its absolute level), so a cell is always about 14 to 28 pixels deep: a streak never thins under about three pixels nor grows into a puddle close up, and far away perspective draws marks out into long thin dashes (past about a hundred metres per cell an even share remains). The lattice is bent by a smooth, bounded warp, marks sit anywhere across their cell's depth, their lengths vary from cell to cell and about half the cells hold none, so they never line up in rows, columns or brick patterns. Each is lit for a fixed share of a slow 2.2 to 5 s cycle and brightens and fades smoothly over it. The facets of the swell and chop (not the fast ripple families, whose facets turned too far between frames) set how bright a lit streak is, within a wide lobe, and marks and sparkles appear only where the sun's reflection falls on the swell's broad shape (a wide lobe on the swell plus a little chop) and while the sun stands out against the sky (none under an overcast or storm sky, where the sheen and halo stay). Their brightness saturates softly below white (0.95 · (1 − e^(−1.6 x)), tinted by the sun), so the path never clips into flickering pure-white pixels. Edges are antialiased through the grid's own screen derivatives and a streak thinner than a pixel dims by its coverage. The marks used to be one- to two-pixel morse-code dashes on a four-times-finer near grid, gated per pixel by fast ripple facets, so about half of their bright pixels changed every frame; earlier still they slid across the water and blinked at random.
  - Shallows: the water column's per-channel transmittance (from Shallow Color; Opacity caps it) tints what lies below turquoise, red first, along the view ray down to the bed and back; drifting caustic lines light the bed under shallow water, faded with depth and before they alias.
  - Foam (Foam Amount scales all but contact foam): a shore band following the waves (reaching at most one and a half Foam Widths beyond the rest shoreline's band, so a flat bar far out stays clear) that breathes with the swash and, over terrain, starts at the swash's edge, riding the run-up and the backwash over the uncovered wet film, while the swash front's soft lace runs up the beach and leaves a stranded line; soft lace lines riding each bore in toward the shore with fading lace behind them and a fainter line halfway between bores (from Medium; Low draws the swash front alone) and one broad soft surf band; contact collars (a thin line at the waterline, then a small lacy wreath at most about a metre wide, its width swelling and narrowing along the waterline with a slow noise, pulsing with the swell at the hull, stretched downwind into a short wake and shedding soft puffs downstream, with a looser ring of lace clinging a moment as the water drops, so it never reads as a sticker's even cut line) with wisps on the first ripple crests, which go out in bursts with each rise; caps on the primary swell's crests where the sea is steep enough to break (**Crest Foam** sets coverage) with ribbons of decaying foam trailing down the wave backs; and, from Medium with **Surface Foam** above 0, long drifting streaks along the wind, denser on the backs of steep waves. One lace pattern cuts all foam: solid where dense and fraying into strands where it thins (two lace octaves and a coarse far octave from Medium; from High holes and a finer octave, the holes stretched along the wind and sized by the lace noise so they streak rather than punching even rounds; Low one soft lace octave), fading to its average coverage before it would alias. Thick foam is bright, its fraying edge thinner and cool, and foam in a wave's shade is blue-grey (warm grey under a warm low sun). Churned water around foam is lighter and greener.
  - Refraction (Medium up, views with a scene copy): the water is opaque and adds the refracted scene through the per-channel transmittance, lit by the caustics, outside the reflection and foam. Object reflections replace the sky by its Fresnel weight, never over foam.
- **Toon** (`toonSource`, `SLATE_WATER_TOON`), in the spirit of The Legend of Zelda: The Wind Waker: a vivid flat palette in hard-edged bands, opaque white foam and brush-stroke line work, a sun drawn as horizontal dashes and far water that steps lighter into a flat horizon tone. Every edge is a hard step antialiased by its own field's screen derivative (`fwidth`), so shapes stay crisp at grazing angles too; strokes keep at least about a pixel and a half and thin out by culling (fewer, shorter dashes) rather than fading, so nothing greys into hairlines or speckle. Every tier is ALU only apart from the copy and reflection features:
  - Light: the strongest directional light (`slateWaterSun`) and hemispheric ambient (`slateWaterLight`) set an exposure (about 0.5-1.15) and a 40% hue tint; the palette itself stays flat. A weak or missing sun (intensity under about 0.85: overcast, storm) greys the palette toward slate blue (foam stays near white) and darkens a steep sea. A low, warm sun (dusk) deepens the sea toward indigo, tints foam gold, turns the sun path gold and the far water rose-violet, warming toward the sun. With no light estimate at all (no sun, ambient at its floor: a scene lit only by an environment), the palette shows as authored.
  - Colour: **Color Bands** split depth into hard-edged bands from Shallow Color through a mid tone (Deep and Shallow mixed 45% in linear) to Deep Color; 0 or 1 band is a smooth gradient. The bed's depth sets them; refraction bounds only the absorption of what shows through. The edge into Deep Color bends with the wide noises (by at most about half a band), so a shelf's straight or boxy contours read as rounded lobes, while the shallower edges stay clean on gentle beds. The shallowest band lets the bed show through (65% opacity × Opacity), and within the shallow bands the wave tones recede. **Color Variation** (0-1) adds wide hard-edged drifts up to 14% lighter over deep water.
  - Waves: two flat tones from the swell's slope (a little chop, exaggerated near the camera) lit relative to flat water: one hard step into a deeper, cooler shade (deeper within a few metres of the eye). Slope across the waves' heading counts 40%, so shaded faces run along the crests instead of tiling as ovals, and the cut drifts with the gust and large noises. Toward the horizon the shade thins in patches of the same noises (to a fifth at most), so far crests band only here and there instead of striping the sea like blinds. Wave tops step to a lighter blue of the same hue near the camera. The chop shapes the tones only within a few metres of the eye, and seen steeply from high up the tones recede to a quarter, leaving the wide Color Variation drifts, so crossing swell components never print a cellular pattern. The Stylized chop octaves drift their phase and, in opposition between neighbours, their strength across the shared noises (as Realistic's do), so close water never forms ribs or a dimple lattice.
  - Lines: white brush strokes that swell from about a third of their width at their ends to their full width (two to four pixels, constant on screen); dashes too short to swell past half their width are dropped, so none shrinks to a speck. Crest lines follow isolines of the swell's crest height alone (on each crest's flanks, near its top, and a trough-side isoline from Medium), bent and broken into dashes by noises seeded per crest: each crest of the swell's dominant component (its steepest slot) has an index that changes in that component's trough, where no line draws, so a crest keeps its dashes as it travels while the next crest's never line up with them into ladders or railway sleepers. The index counts whole periods of the component's world phase: the CPU reduces the phase uniform mod 2π, so it wraps once a period as the clock runs, and passes the periods it removed (mod 4096) in `slateWaterLight.w`, so the index never jumps. (Without that count every crest took the next crest's dashes at each wrap, so the line work changed all at once every one to three seconds.) Each isoline has its own dash noise and a slow wobble of its level (±0.06), so a crest's isolines never run as ruled parallel triplets. The top isoline closes round each crest, so it keeps only its longer dashes and only where it runs along the crest (its height's gradient within about 50° of the dominant heading): arcs, never whole rings. On a low sea state (calm) the top isoline thins out as crests flatten and the trough isoline goes, so a calm sea draws one isoline. Their cut rises where the swell's slope along the dominant heading is slight (under about 0.05-0.16 of the dominant component's k·Wave Height: the flat tops and bottoms of the wave groups' isolated bumps, where an isoline would close into a whole ring that pops in at full size as the bump's peak crosses its level), where the field is too steep to hold a width, with the footprint, and close to the eye where a crest (at the primary swell's phase speed) would sweep more than about ten pixels between frames, so they never strobe across the view.
