Within several metres of the eye, short curled strokes ride the water itself (its rest position, so they bob with the waves, drifting with the current and slowly downwind): isolines of a noise stretched along the crests, with a lighter dapple tone on the same noise's highs; a slow noise drifting through them shortens some and grows others in over a few seconds. From Medium their scale steps with the footprint in octaves (two neighbouring octaves cross over by culling, so their density on screen stays steady); Low draws one fixed scale of about a metre. Crest lines used to include the chop's crests and be cut by an isotropic noise, and the strokes rode the chop's dragged domain, so both changed shape and popped between frames. Strokes keep about six pixels clear of every isoline, so they never cross a crest line into X and V shapes. Strokes and flecks give way in the sun's column and around object contacts, so those keep the eye: the column is measured on the swell alone and widened (with a weaker halo around it), and raises their cuts, so they shorten and vanish as whole shapes there instead of being perforated pixel by pixel by the chop's normal.
  - **Subsurface** (0-2) lightens the wave-top tone and, from Medium, looking toward a sun some way up (not overhead), steps the slope just under each crest to a brighter blue.
  - Sun: one flat colour (a warm cream by day, warmer than the cool off-white line work, gold at dusk) in horizontal dashes down the reflection column. The column comes from the swell's normal with some chop (width from **Roughness**, plus that normal's variation across the pixel under the same capped pixel filter as Realistic's, `PIXEL_SLOPE_FILTER`; narrower on Low), so near the eye it breaks with the waves without jittering from pixel to pixel. The dashes lie on world rows across the sun's azimuth, bent by the waves (by a world distance, limited by the waves' slope so a row never folds back into chevrons with hairline combs) and cut by a noise along them that drifts at a fixed world speed; toward the column's core they lengthen and thicken, and near the eye they are thinner and some rows thin out. Only a dash's thickness follows the camera, continuously: rows stay 7 to 14 pixels apart by culling (as the footprint grows every other row thins out and vanishes, and the rows that stay never move), each row's dashes are seeded by its world index, and dash lengths step with the footprint by shrinking one noise octave's dashes away while the next octave's grow in. Power-of-two pitches and lengths used to switch per pixel, so whole dashes popped as the camera moved. Strokes and flecks give way in the column (see Lines). From High with **Sparkles** above 0, a few large four-pointed stars (10-16 px, upright on screen) twinkle in the column's core: each cell's world offset is solved into pixels through the footprint, and they shrink to fit cells only a few pixels wide.
  - Horizon: far water steps lighter twice (halfway, then all the way) to a far tone (Deep and Shallow mixed 30% and lifted by day; rose-violet at dusk, warming to the sun's gold toward its azimuth; greyed with the weather). The steps measure how steeply the view meets the swell's own surface (a third of its slope) at about 0.1 and 0.045, so wave backs turned toward the horizon step lighter first and the step edges run along the swell and travel with it. The wide noises shift both thresholds, so their edges break into islands and peninsulas rather than running as one even shelf around the camera or as corduroy rows of a single swell, and where the swell is no longer resolved they soften only slightly (a gradient used to leave the flat palette). Over the last few degrees the far tone fades gradually into a horizon tone that only hides detail, so no brighter seam runs along it: the sky's own colour just above the horizon where the view has a scene copy (Medium up, two copy taps, four from High; less of it at dusk), otherwise the far tone. The steps used to measure the view ray alone, bent by world noises: hard bands fixed to the view, with lens-shaped islands sliding through them as the camera moved, and a flat strip of far tone between the far water and the sky. There is no sky reflection apart from object reflections.
  - Foam (Foam Amount scales all but contact foam): opaque white in the scene's light (at least 90%), a cool white on shaded wave faces, gold-tinted at dusk; no outline. Shore: a thick band on the waterline (2.3 Foam Widths; 1.2 along a body's own edge over open water, such as a lake's bank) whose edge runs as billowed scallops (a noise about two Foam Widths across folded at its middle value: rounded lobes meeting in sharp cusps; Medium up, Low uses the medium noise) and runs in and out with the swash. Over terrain the band is cut hard where the backwash has uncovered the shallows, the bore's front runs up the beach as a bold line that thins (never fades) as it drains, and one flat, dark neutral wet tone with a hard edge marks the film that is still wet, shrinking back as the sand dries. From Medium a bold line rides each bore in toward the band, thinning as it arrives; Low keeps one ring breathing in and out about one Foam Width beyond the band. Object contacts: a collar whose radius swells and shrinks gently (±12%) with a slow noise about one and a quarter Contact Foam Widths across (so it never opens holes at the waterline) and pulses with the swell at the hull (out as the water climbs, clinging a moment as it drops), antialiased by the pixel footprint rather than the contact field's stepped screen derivatives (which spiked its edge into hairs), one bold ring breathing with it just outside, a thinner ring sent out with each rise (on the clock for flat water) that thins and breaks up as it goes, and from Medium round puffs shed downstream. Crest caps where steep crests fold (crest sharpness and the Gerstner Jacobian, broken along the crest; **Crest Foam** sets coverage, and the default 0.35 leaves only a few small caps on a moderate sea), only on the upper part of each wave so they run along the crests, whose cut rises with the footprint so distant caps shrink and vanish; from Medium with **Surface Foam** above 0, drifting patches stretched along the crests on the backs of steep waves. Patches, and caps where Crest Foam is above about 0.5, open round holes of varied size, larger toward their edges: below High in about half the cells of a jittered world grid (one cell, no neighbour search), from High at the nearest of a 3 × 3 neighbourhood of freely placed points (stretched a little along the wind, sizes and openings from noises), so holes never sit in rows and columns; the cell size steps with the footprint so holes stay a few pixels or more. On every tier, small oval flecks (rounded: diamonds read as glyphs) on two jittered grids riding the water with the strokes near the eye; each lives for part of a slow cycle, growing from a point to its full size and shrinking back, and drops out by its own rank (shrinking away, never switching off) before its full size would be under about three pixels, and close up none grows past about a dozen pixels. Their rank cut used to follow their pulsing size, so they popped on and off between frames. No Voronoi cell pattern cuts the foam below High.
  - Shore distance: the body's own bank and, over known terrain, the field's stored distance to the terrain shoreline at rest. The shore band follows the waves where the depth is nearer (as if the bed shelved 1:10), but reaches at most 0.6 Foam Widths beyond the rest shoreline's band, so a flat shallow bar far from the shore stays clear. The outer ring moves along the rest distance alone, so it never flashes across a flat bar; it keeps at least about a pixel and a half.
  - Refraction (Medium up, views with a scene copy) shows the bed through Opacity: what shows through takes the water's hue at a gain of at most 1 and fades toward the water's own colour with the refracted depth. Object reflections replace the water by a Fresnel × Reflection Strength weight, never over foam.
- New Water assets and components without an asset use the retuned presets. The Realistic preset was retuned for its open-ocean look with clear tropical shallows: Shallow Color 0.1/0.68/0.95 (azure, so clear shallows over sand read turquoise and azure while light through crests glows teal), Deep Color 0.03/0.15/0.3, Roughness 0.04, Depth Color Distance 6.5 m. Stylized defaults follow the look (`createDefaultWaterDefinition(style, look)`); they differ only in these fields:

  | Field | Painted | Toon |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | Shallow Color | 0.1 / 0.72 / 0.66 | 0.2 / 0.86 / 0.9 |
  | Deep Color | 0.04 / 0.21 / 0.31 | 0.05 / 0.27 / 0.7 |
  | Foam Color | 0.97 / 0.99 / 1 | 1 / 1 / 1 |
  | Reflection Strength | 1 | 0.8 |
  | Depth Color Distance | 4 m | 6 m |
  | Color Bands | 0 | 3 |
  | Sparkles | 0.5 | 0.4 |

  Both keep Opacity 1, Foam Width 1.2 m, Subsurface 1 and Color Variation 0.5. Saved assets keep their stored values; older assets without Surface Foam, Subsurface or the fields below take their style's defaults (and Stylized Look Painted's), so Water stays asset version 1 without a migration. Existing assets therefore gain Gerstner motion (Steepness 0.5 Realistic, 0.3 Stylized); set Steepness 0 for the previous vertical-only waves.
- Asset fields added for Water v2 (Realistic / Stylized defaults). Physics fields change queries and buoyancy; render-only fields never do:

  | Field | Range | Defaults | Use |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | Steepness | 0-1 | 0.5 / 0.3 | Physics. Gerstner horizontal motion. |
  | Wave Model | Classic, Ocean Spectrum | Classic | Physics. Source of the swell components. |
  | Peak Sharpness | 1-7 | 3.3 | Physics, Ocean Spectrum only. JONSWAP peak enhancement γ. |
  | Wave Seed | 0-65535 | 0 | Physics, Ocean Spectrum only. Deterministic component draw. |
  | Detail Waves | 0-1 | 1 / 0 | Render only. Gain of the FFT detail band above either model's swell, sampled where FFT Ocean Detail runs (High and Ultra): per-pixel slopes and Jacobian foam, plus small displaced waves where a GPU-wave mesh resolves a whole cascade (Ultra's first cascade on Global Water's dense core). 0 compiles it out and runs no band. |
  | Refraction | 0-1 | 0.35 / 0.15 | Render only. How far the waves bend the refracted scene (Medium and up, on views with a scene copy). 0 keeps the blended surface and never samples the copy. |
  | Object Reflections | On/Off | On / Off | Render only. Reflect scene objects: the screen-space march at High, the planar mirror for the dominant flat body at Ultra. Off reflects only the sky. |
  | Color Variation | 0-1 | 0.5 | Render only. Open-sea tint variation. |
  | Stylized Look | Painted, Toon | Painted | Render only, Stylized only. Which Stylized shader the water compiles (`SLATE_WATER_TOON`); switching keeps colours and wave settings. |
- Planar reflection owner (`water-planar-reflection.ts`; built-in water samples it as described under refraction and object reflections above):
  - `waterPlanarReflectionForCamera(scene, camera)` returns `{ texture, viewProjection, planeY, mesh, gammaSpace }` for the reflection that camera's view drew in this scene render, or null. Each lookup also requests the reflection for the view's next renders: the first returns null and later renders return the drawn result. Water must look it up on every render it draws; after 4 of the view's renders without a lookup the view stops drawing. The object is reused; check `mesh` before sampling.
  - Only scenes retained by `retainWaterPlanarReflections` (every `SceneRenderCoordinator` view) draw one. Previews, thumbnails, Render Target Captures and the mirror pass itself get null.
  - It draws only while device-effective Reflections are Planar and an eligible body is visible. Eligible means built-in water with **Object Reflections** on, flat (not a river or a volume tilted out of level, the same `restVaries` test as WaterField) and below the eye. One body per view is chosen: the largest projected rest rectangle, then the nearer plane, then the older mesh.
  - Views that always get null (water keeps its fallback): orthographic, rig/XR, more than one active camera, clustered-light views, Scene Linear without half-float render targets, and an eye less than 1 mm above the plane. The last device case is also a device clamp: `clampWaterQualityToDevice(quality, caps, { sceneLinear })` reports Sky Only there (its Screen Space fallback needs the half-float copy too), so Play readback says Clamped instead of Planar.
  - A rigid camera at the eye mirrored across the rest plane (view x flipped, so winding and back-face culling are unchanged) renders the opaque scene. The water plane is an oblique near plane frozen into its projection (full-range, half-range and reverse depth), so submerged geometry never reflects and no material compiles a clip-plane variant.
  - The pass is drawn capture-style from `onBeforeRender` with `drawBorrowedTarget`, under the Render Target Capture readiness exemption. It never enters `scene.customRenderTargets` and works with every FrameGraph view. The mirror camera and target never join `scene.cameras` or `scene.textures`, so allocating, freeing and reallocating storage adds no strict readiness probe and drops no presented frame.
  - The render list is culled by the mirrored frustum and the water plane. It skips water, editor helpers, lines, alpha-blended meshes, particles and sprites. Level of detail follows the view camera.
  - `viewProjection` takes positions relative to `slateWaterOrigin` (the view's eye under floating origin); its eye-relative translation is formed in float64. A water fragment at clip position c samples uv = 0.5 + 0.5·c.xy/c.w on WebGL2 and WebGPU alike (Babylon's WebGPU engine stores render-target rows in WebGL's order). `water-planar-reflection.test.ts` checks the mirrored projection, the near plane at the water and eye-relative precision far from the origin.
  - Colour keeps the view's image-processing setting, so reflected materials reuse the view's shader variants. Scene Linear views store linear RGBA16F; display views store display-encoded 8-bit colour (`gammaSpace: true`, decode with `toLinearSpace`). On WebGPU the target matches the view's formats (BGRA on a bgra8unorm swap chain, stencil depth), so reflected draws reuse its render pipelines. Alpha is coverage: 0 where nothing was reflected, so a shader can keep its sky reflection there.
  - The target is Planar Resolution × the view size, snapped up to 8 px, with depth. It is leased in the managed ledger's `water` category before allocation and resizes in place, reserving the new size before releasing the old charge.
  - It redraws on every render while the view moves and every other render while it is static. Every window counts renders, never engine frames, so frame caps, fast displays and paused views keep their reflection. Storage is freed after 120 scene renders unused (the view keeps its storage-free mirror camera and target), and everything is released at once when Reflections leave Planar. A failed draw logs one warning and disables planar reflections until Water quality changes.
- Wave model (core `water.ts`, shared by the renderer, the game worker and the editor):
  - `waterWaveSet(definition)` caches each definition's scale-independent components (k, ω, heading, relative amplitude, phase, S₁ = Σk·A) and the warp below. It rebuilds only when a wave field changes and never keys on Wave Scale, which scripts and handles change live.
  - Classic is the eight-component table (`waterWaveComponents`): its energy is spread over the band like a sea spectrum (no component carries more than about a third of it), at incommensurate frequencies (0.78-3.61 × the peak) and headings fanned to both sides of Wave Direction; the three longest components cross at up to 0.77 rad per unit of 2 × Wave Spread (they were within 0.47), so far water, where filtering leaves only them, reads as short crossing crests rather than long parallel rows. The frequencies are spaced so the deep-water angular frequencies (∝ √frequency) never come back into step: the slope-weighted autocorrelation Σ (k·a)²·cos(ω·τ) stays under 0.24 from its first zero to five peak periods (the previous table re-aligned to 0.33 about one period later, the ~2 s beat Painted showed on short Wave Lengths). The earlier five-component table put 70% of the energy in one wave train, so the sea printed parallel rows and a regular lattice; Σ a² is unchanged, so its significant height is too. Ocean Spectrum draws 8 components from a deep-water JONSWAP spectrum: stratified log-wavenumbers over 0.7-4 × the peak, with Mulberry32 (`createSeededRng(waveSeed)`) jitter and phases. Amplitudes are normalized to Classic's significant height (Hs ≈ 1.69 × Wave Height), so switching models keeps the sea's size. Wave Length is the peak wavelength, Wave Direction the heading of the strongest components, Wave Spread the cos² heading spread (0 is one heading), Wave Speed the time scale, Choppiness the crest profile.
  - Both models share a travelling domain warp (`waterSwellWarp`): every component is evaluated at u = x0 + W(x0, t), W = Σ A·K̂·cos(K·x0 + φ − ν·t) over three terms at about 0.1-0.15 × the peak wavenumber (crests curve over about eight Wave Lengths) with A = 0.26 × Wave Length, headed from Wave Direction. At 0.2 Wave Lengths crest rows still ran parallel across a wide view; at 0.26 the strongest off-axis autocorrelation of the swell's slope beyond a wavelength falls from 0.28 to 0.20 on a 22 m swell (0.35 to 0.27 on a 9 m calm) and its sharpest spatial spectral peak about fourfold (top-down 256 × 256 maps over 16 Wave Lengths). W is a gradient field, so M = I + ∂W/∂x0 is symmetric; rest-space derivatives carry it (∇₀H = M·∇ᵤH, ∇₀D = ∇ᵤD·M, so `jacobianXZ` and `jacobianZX` now differ everywhere). A finite set of plane waves is quasi-periodic, and with few of them the eye sees the lattice; the warp bends and shifts it from place to place, so no stretch of sea repeats another. Measured on the kernel itself (top-down 256 m maps, mean of the five strongest secondary autocorrelation peaks): Classic 0.99 → 0.50 at Wave Length 12 m (the old table repeated almost exactly every 102 m), 0.96 → 0.50 at 22 m; Ocean Spectrum 0.88 → 0.47. It is independent of quality, so physics, the GPU vertex stage and every fragment evaluate the same surface (`evaluateWaterSwellWarp`, `waterSwellWarpShaderConstants`).
    - The warp travels: ν = 0.027, 0.061 and 0.041 × the peak's angular frequency √(g·2π / Wave Length) · Wave Speed (each term once every 16 to 37 peak periods; its displacement changes at a few percent of the crests' speed, so nothing swims, and Wave Speed 0 freezes it with the waves). With a static warp each point's motion was a fixed sum of eight periodic components, so a still view came back to nearly the same sea every 20 to 50 s (frame correlation 0.55 on the kernel's slope field); the travelling warp keeps shifting the components' relative phases, so it never does (0.28 or less over 120 s). The rates satisfy 3ν₀ − 2ν₁ + ν₂ = 0, so the FFT band's tile blend (that combination of the terms' phases) stays fixed in the world. The kernel's ∂H/∂t and ∂D/∂t include the moving evaluation point (∇ᵤH·∂W/∂t and −S·∂W/∂t), so queried velocities still match the moving surface; `waterWaveDrift` and `waterSurfaceDrift` take the time for the warp's local stretch, and `waterSwellWarpShaderConstants(set, originX, originZ, out, time)` reduces −ν·t into each term's phase in float64 (no GPU cost).
    - `warpStretch` is 1 + the largest eigenvalue ∂W/∂x0 reaches (about 1.37): no local wavevector is longer than that, so mesh and footprint filters fade a component at `frequency · warpStretch`. A mesh therefore keeps every Classic component at full height (and matches physics queries exactly) only with cells under about Wave Length / 20: Medium's default Global Water fades the shortest by about 3%, and a finite body at Surface Resolution 48 on Medium drops the shortest two (the second to about 1% of its height) and fades the next two (to about 40% and 90%). High's default Global Water resolves them all.
    - `jacobianSlope` = S₁ + Σₜ Aₜ·Kₜ·Σᵢ kᵢ·Aᵢ·(dᵢ·K̂ₜ)² is the largest Σ k·A·dᵀ·M·d anywhere; q divides by it instead of S₁ (see q below), so the warp never folds the surface. At the defaults that is about 31% more than S₁, and the eight-component table's S₁ is about 37% more than the five-component one's, so Steepness gives about 40% less horizontal motion per component than before (about 20% less in total crest sharpening).
  - Wave groups (`waterWaveGroups`, both models): each component's height is modulated by g = (1 + m·cos(κ·ê·u − Ω·t + ψ)) / √(1 + m²/2), an envelope 9 to 17 of its wavelengths long (κ = 0.06-0.11 × k), turned 0.7-1.2 rad off the component's heading and travelling at its deep-water group velocity (Ω = κ·cos(turn)·ω / 2k, half the crest speed), depth m 0.65-0.85: each component swells and nearly vanishes in its own wide patches, so different headings lead in different places and a wide view never reads as one even texture of parallel rows (the earlier groups, seven wavelengths long and m 0.3-0.5, left far water uniform). Crests run through each group, growing and fading as they pass, so the sea comes in sets with calmer water between them and no crest shape returns to the same place after a period; the √ keeps each component's mean energy (significant height and mean drift unchanged). Only the height carries the envelope: the Gerstner offset stays unmodulated, so the Jacobian bound and Steepness's crest sharpening are unchanged. The kernel's ∇H and ∂H/∂t include the envelope's own derivatives; `heightSum` (Σ A·(1 + m)/√(1 + m²/2)) bounds |H| for `waterWaveEnvelope`, while `amplitudeSum` keeps the horizontal bound. Worker physics, CPU meshes and both shader stages evaluate the same envelope from `waterWaveShaderConstants`' third vec4 per component (κê.x, κê.z, group phase reduced in float64 against the floating origin, m); the fragment adds the envelope's slope from High up (about a sixteenth of the crest's). (The sea used to recur visibly: with the fragment's three components on Low the picture came back almost exactly every three seconds, and a calm sea's every two. The groups weaken these returns; the respaced Classic frequencies and the travelling warp remove the rest.)
  - Surges (`waterWaveSurges`, `waterWaveSurge`, both models): each component's phase gains s(t) = β·(sin(Ωa·t + ψa) + sin(Ωb·t + ψb)), the same everywhere at one time, with β = 1.1 × min(1, relative wavenumber / 2) and Ωa, Ωb at 7-15% of the component's own ω. Its crests surge ahead and linger (by 9% of their speed for the longest component, up to 28% for the short ones that carry the slope), on tones in irrational ratios to each other and to every other component's, so a still view's components keep drifting out of step instead of coming back in step a few seconds later. Being uniform in space a surge changes no wavevector: slopes, the Jacobian and its Gerstner bound are untouched. The kernel's phase gains s(t) and its rates use ω − ∂s/∂t; `waterWaveShaderConstants` folds s(t) into each phase uniform (no GPU cost), and Toon's crest count and the shore swash clock follow the surged lead component. `waterWaveSurge` caches the values per wave set for the last time asked, so a tick's evaluations compute them once. On the kernel's slope at 400 points over 20 s, the strongest frame autocorrelation beyond 2 s falls from 0.36 to 0.32 (22 m swell), 0.10 to 0.085 (9 m calm) and 0.32 to 0.31 (storm), and the calm sea's strongest temporal line from 40% to 32% of the slope's power. Mean drift (`waterWaveDrift`) uses each component's surged ω at the query's time; a support coupled loosely to the water (1/s) in a storm still drifts about 2 cm/s, of the 4.5 cm/s it would without the drift correction, because the surges modulate the frequencies it lags behind.
  - Both models share one detail spectrum: `cutoffK` (4 × the peak wavenumber, above Classic's shortest swell at 3.67 ×), `spectrumScale` and `waterOceanSpectrumDensity` give the render-only FFT band the analytic band's normalization, so the bands never double count. Ocean Spectrum uses its Peak Sharpness; Classic uses the JONSWAP shape with γ 3.3, scaled so its analytic band holds the Classic swell's variance. `detailHeight` is the significant height above the cutoff.
  - `evaluateWaterWaves(set, x0, z0, t, spacing, out, scale, gain, gainX, gainZ)` evaluates forward at a rest point without allocating: height H, Gerstner offset D = gain · Σ q·a·d·cos p, ∇₀H, the Jacobian J = I + gain·∇₀D + D ⊗ ∇₀gain (`jacobianXZ` and `jacobianZX` differ only inside a bank fade), ∂H/∂t and ∂D/∂t (`WaterWaveSlot`). `waterEulerianGradient` applies J⁻ᵀ. q = Steepness · min(3.5, 0.9 / (jacobianSlope · Wave Scale)) is computed per call. Det J never drops below 0.1 (det(I − S·M) = det(I − M^½·S·M^½) ≥ 1 − q·jacobianSlope), so the surface never folds; horizontal motion scales with amplitude, so calm water never slides sideways; small or gentle waves get proportionally less sharpening.
  - Queries invert world X/Z to the rest point (`invertWaterWaves`: a Picard start plus at most 4 Newton steps, stopping within 1 µm; a fixed cap so every host agrees; a caller with a nearby rest point may pass it as the start), then intersect the volume's rest base there. Normals are J⁻ᵀ(∇rest + ∇H). Steepness 0 skips the inversion and evaluates the vertical-only (warped) waves exactly.
  - Finite bodies fade the horizontal offset to zero at their banks over 4 horizontal envelopes (`waterBankFadeLength`, `waterBankGain`). The mesh, queries (which sample the fade at each inversion step near a bank; blended queries read it through a stencil, below) and the built-in shader share it, so edges stay on the authored footprint against walls, pools and docks, and the displaced surface covers exactly the rest footprint. Global Water Volume has no banks.
  - `waterWaveEnvelope(definition, scale)` bounds |H| (`heightSum`: each amplitude at the top of its wave group, plus 4σ of the detail band × Detail Waves, for both models); `waterHorizontalEnvelope` bounds |D| (q · Σ amplitudes, which carry no groups). They size the contact layers, culling bounds and bank fade. With eight components at the tops of their (now deeper) wave groups the analytic |H| bound is about 2.2 × the five-component table's, so contact layers spread further from rest and culling bounds pad further than before.
  - `waterWaveShaderConstants(set, scale, originX, originZ, time, out)` writes 12 floats per component for GPU evaluation (the third vec4 its wave group), with the phases `(k·d·origin − ω·t + φ) mod 2π` and `(κê·origin − Ω·t + ψ) mod 2π` reduced in float64 so shaders never take large-argument sines on mobile GPUs. `waterSwellWarpShaderConstants(set, originX, originZ, out, time)` writes the warp's terms the same way, (K.x, K.z, A / |K|, phase at `time`), one vec4 each.
  - Mean drift (`waterWaveDrift`, `waterSurfaceDrift`): the warp scales each component's Eulerian mean by dᵀ·M·d at the point, so both take an optional world X/Z and time and use that point's M then; `sampleWaterSurface` and buoyancy pass the query or support position and the clock, so a support anywhere still rocks in place.
- GPU vertex waves (built-in shading, `SLATE_WATER_GPU_WAVES`): the mesh keeps a static rest grid and the vertex shader displaces it at `CUSTOM_VERTEX_UPDATE_WORLDPOS`, so frames upload no vertex data; only the clock and phases advance, as uniforms.
  - Per vertex it evaluates the kernel forward at its world rest point (eye-relative `worldPos`, against phases reduced relative to the floating origin), unrolled over the swell uniforms: H and the Gerstner offset D with the mesh-spacing filter, and the bank fade from its bank distance. It adds (Dx, H, Dz) to the world position and `vPositionW`; the clip position, fog, shadow coordinates and clip planes follow.
  - Static inputs reuse the existing attributes: `slateWaterData` = (mesh spacing to the farthest neighbour, bank distance, depth, 0); positions and normals are the rest grid and rest normal (the fragment derives its own normal from the swell). `slateWaterOffset` stays zero.
  - `CUSTOM_VERTEX_MAIN_END` runs after that hook, so it writes the computed H to `vSlateWater.x` and D to `vSlateWaterFlow.zw` instead of copying the attributes.
  - `evaluateWaterVertex` (core) is the CPU reference of exactly this evaluation. The CPU path calls it, and unit tests check that the uploaded rest grid, spacing and bank distances reproduce physics queries through it.
  - Vertex-stage spherical irradiance (foam diffuse only) sees the rest normal. Passes that compile the material itself (its depth pre-pass, FrameGraph geometry rendering) see the displaced surface. Babylon's `DepthRenderer` (Scene Depth for post-process materials) draws with its own vertex shader and sees the rest grid; water never casts shadows.
  - The shared outline mask (selection, Outline components, CEL outlines) adds the same displacement: `waterOutlineVertexSource` injects it after the selection shader's `worldPos` under `SLATE_WATER_GPU_WAVES`, and `gpuWaterWaves(material, mesh)` makes the mask program declare it and bind the material's swell for that frame (`WaterMaterialPlugin.bindVertexWaves`). Outlines therefore trace the drawn waves, and a CEL boat's outline is cut at the moving waterline. Where the material's vertex stage samples the FFT detail band, `vertexWaveDefines()` adds `SLATE_WATER_FFT_VERTEX` and `bindVertexWaves` binds the same band (see FFT detail in built-in water below). CPU-path water already uploads displaced vertices and compiles no displacement.
  - CPU picking (`scene.pick`, marquee selection and brushes) intersects the rest grid, within the wave envelope of the drawn surface; viewport taps hit the drawn surface through the GPU pick; gameplay water queries use `sampleWaterSurface`.
- CPU path (Custom Material water, and `setWaterGpuWaves(mesh, false)` for parity captures): each vertex evaluates `evaluateWaterVertex` and the mesh adds (Dx, H, Dz) in local space; it never inverts. It also uploads Eulerian normals, `slateWaterData` = (H, bank distance, depth, time) and, for built-in shading, each vertex's world X/Z offset (`slateWaterOffset`, skipped while calm water stays at zero). Switching a built-in surface between paths rewrites its vertex data and skips drawing it until the new shader variant is ready, so the previous variant never displaces the new layout.
- No browser test compares the GPU and CPU wave paths.
- Culling bounds are the rest extents padded by both envelopes (horizontally only for finite bodies, which covers tilted volumes), so GPU-displaced vertices stay inside frustum culling, contact and terrain fields and removal selection; the bounds stay fixed while waves animate and no per-frame extents pass runs. While a GPU-path surface's material compiles the FFT detail band, finite bodies also pad horizontally by the band's bound, Steepness · `detailHeight` · Detail Waves · Wave Scale (re-padded once when the Water quality changes it); the vertical envelope already includes the band.
- Built-in shading evaluates the same swell per pixel:
  - `waterWaveShaderConstants` fills 24 vec4 uniforms (`slateWaterSwellDir0-7`, `slateWaterSwellAmp0-7`, `slateWaterSwellGroup0-7`) and `waterSwellWarpShaderConstants` three more (`slateWaterSwellWarp0-2`) each bind, relative to the floating origin. `slateWaterSwellInfo` carries the bank fade length, whether Gerstner motion is on, Color Variation and the sea state (below).
  - The vertex stage and the outline mask evaluate all eight components at the warped rest point (3 cos for W), each with its wave group's envelope (one more cos per component). Both wave models compile the same program (Wave Model is uniform-only). The fragment evaluates the warp with its gradient (3 cos, 3 sin) on every tier and the components (each with its envelope, one cos; from High also the envelope's slope, one sin) by descending slope: Low the first 3, Medium 6, High and Ultra all 8. The CPU sums the skipped slots for the compiled tier into `slateWaterSea` (z, w), which count as resolved slope and Realistic roughness, so the sun path widens instead of losing them.
  - Sea state (`swSeaState`, `slateWaterSwellInfo.w`): the looks' thresholds (breaking, wind streaks, Jacobian foam, roughness caps, Toon's storm gloom, the slope variance cap and chop gain in `slateWaterTerms`) read 2.114 · √Σ(k·a)², which equals the five-component table's Σ k·a, so seas read as steep as they were tuned however many components share the slope. Resolved-slope ratios stay on Σ k·a.
  - The vertex stage packs the current and the offset (computed on the GPU path, uploaded on the CPU path) into `vSlateWaterFlow` (current in xy, offset in zw; no extra varying). The fragment's rest point is its eye-relative position minus the interpolated offset, which is exact for the displaced triangles.
  - The swell slope gets J⁻ᵀ, with the bank fade gradient from screen derivatives of the bank distance. Jacobian foam raises crest sharpness to (1 − det J) / 0.9 where Gerstner crests gather water.
  - Normals, whitecaps, subsurface and two-tone shading therefore match the geometry, queries and buoyancy for both wave models. Phases arrive reduced in float64, so mobile GPUs never take large-argument sines of the swell.
- FFT ocean detail (`water-fft.ts`, pure half in `water-fft-spectrum.ts`) simulates the render-only band above the analytic swell. Physics, queries and buoyancy never read it. Built-in water samples it (FFT detail in built-in water, below).
  - Spectrum: wavenumbers above `cutoffK` from `waterOceanSpectrumDensity`, for both wave models. Headings spread around Wave Direction like the analytic components (Wave Spread, at least 0.3), drawn in each cascade's turned frame (Output, below). Each cascade draws from Mulberry32 seeded by Wave Seed and its index. Each cascade's variance is exactly ∫S(k)dk over its band. Bands never overlap, and nothing below the cutoff is synthesized, so no wavenumber counts twice.
  - Unit spectrum: the stored h0 is the band at peak wavenumber 1 and density scale 1. In grid bins it does not depend on Wave Height, Wave Length, Wave Speed or the wave model's scale. The layout scales it by √(spectrumScale · peakK), sets the patch sizes, and runs its clock at √peakK · Wave Speed. Only Wave Direction, Wave Spread, Peak Sharpness, Wave Seed, FFT Size and FFT Cascades draw a new spectrum (`spectrumKey`).
  - Cascades: cascade 0 starts its band N/16 bins from its centre at the cutoff. Each further patch is `waterFftCascadeRatio(cascades)` times smaller and takes the next band: 3 + 2√2 with two cascades, 1 + √2 with three (irrational, so cascades never repeat in step). The last runs to its Nyquist radius, 8× its start. Two and three cascades therefore span the same band, from the cutoff to about 47× it (6.4 cm at Wave Length 12 m). Ultra's third cascade splits High's band more finely, each part on a patch twice High's, instead of adding centimetre ripples that only a footprint within arm's length resolves. Consumers fade each cascade by its shortest wavelength, so a narrower first cascade keeps the band's longer waves, and their vertex displacement, where High's wide one is already gone. At Wave Length 12 m the patches are 12 m (64², 1 cascade), 24 and 4.1 m (128², 2), and 48, 19.9 and 8.2 m (256², 3).
  - Time: unit frequencies are whole multiples of 2π / 256, so the band repeats every 256 / timeScale seconds and the GPU phase is 2π · fract(m · fract(timeScale · t / 256)), with no large arguments in any session length.
  - Passes: a fragment-shader FFT through EffectWrapper/EffectRenderer, the same on WebGL2 and WebGPU (no compute shaders). One time-evolution pass, log2 N horizontal and log2 N − 1 vertical radix-2 Stockham passes (natural order, no bit-reversal pass) over an RGBA32F atlas that holds every cascade, then the last vertical pass once per output layer: 2·log2 N + 2·cascades draws (14 at 64²×1, 18 at 128²×2, 22 at 256²×3), all texel fetches.
    - Precision: pairs of real fields share one complex transform, and GPU rounding in one half leaks into the other. ∂Dx/∂z, up to 100 times larger than Dz in the smallest cascade, therefore travels divided by its cascade's mean wavenumber (`shearScales`) and the final pass multiplies it back; without that, Dz was 1.5% off at 256²×3. Phases use m = 64·mHigh + mLow, so float32 keeps fract(m · cycle) to about 1e-4 rad for m up to about 4200.
  - Output: `waterFftForSurface(scene, definition)` returns `{ texture, cascades, patchSizes, amplitudeGain, ready }` or null. `definition` is the surface's normalized definition (`WaterMaterialPlugin.water`).
    - `texture` is one 2D array, N × N × 2·cascades, RGBA16F (RGBA32F where half-float targets do not render), bilinear, repeat, no mips.
    - Layer 2c holds (Dx, H, Dz, ∂Dx/∂z) of cascade c and layer 2c + 1 holds (∂H/∂x, ∂H/∂z, ∂Dx/∂x, ∂Dz/∂z), in metres and slopes at Detail Waves 1, Wave Scale 1 and horizontal scale λ = 1. The offset follows the analytic Gerstner sign (water gathers under crests).
    - Each cascade's texture frame is turned from the world's by its own angle (`WATER_FFT_CASCADE_TURNS`: 0°, 31° and 59°), and its spectrum is drawn about Wave Direction minus that angle, so the waves still run along Wave Direction once turned back. The cascades' periodic patches therefore tile along different axes and never line up into one rectangular repeat.
    - Texel (i, j) of cascade c is p = (i, j) · L_c / N (mod L_c) in its turned frame p = R(−θ_c)·(X, Z): sample at p / L_c + 0.5 / N, adding `fract(R(−θ_c)·origin / L_c)` from the CPU under a floating origin, and turn the outputs back: slope and offset by R(θ_c), the ∂D tensor by R(θ_c)·∂D·R(θ_c)ᵀ.
  - Consumer contract (built-in water follows it):
    - Multiply every channel by g = `amplitudeGain` (Detail Waves) · Wave Scale. H and the slopes are then final.
    - Scale Dx, Dz and the three ∂D terms by λ = Steepness · bank gain (`waterBankGain` over the body's `waterBankFadeLength`), never the raw λ = 1, so Steepness 0 bodies and banks get no horizontal detail, as with the analytic swell.
    - The detail Jacobian is J = I + λ·g·[[∂Dx/∂x, ∂Dx/∂z], [∂Dx/∂z, ∂Dz/∂z]]. Add its terms to the analytic Jacobian and keep the combined determinant at or above `WATER_JACOBIAN_FLOOR` (lower λ locally), which the rest-point inversion and Jacobian foam assume.
    - Bounds: |H| and |D| / λ stay statistically within 4σ = `detailHeight` · Detail Waves · Wave Scale. `waterWaveEnvelope` already includes it vertically. `waterHorizontalEnvelope` and the bank fade, which physics shares, do not; a consumer that displaces geometry horizontally pads its culling bounds by λ times that 4σ.
    - Call `waterFftForSurface` every frame the material samples the band. The call is the demand that keeps the simulation running.
  - Lifecycle: one simulation per scene and band. Equal waves, Wave Seed and Water quality share it, and each definition keeps its own `amplitudeGain`.
    - It exists only while device-effective FFT Ocean Detail is on, a material asked for the band in the last two observer runs, and visible built-in water with Detail Waves above 0 requests it from the water before-render observer (`requestWaterFft`: enabled, visible, and active by the observer's own test: drawn last frame or inside an active camera's frustum, so the request adds no second frustum test). Water that no material samples costs nothing.
    - `updateSceneWaterFft` dispatches it at most once per engine frame and only when the water clock moved, so captures and paused or between-tick Play frames add no passes. Wave Speed 0 bands are drawn once.
    - New spectra are drawn on the CPU in budgeted steps (`WATER_FFT_BUILD_TEXELS` = 16384 texels per observer run, about 1 ms on desktop; 1, 4 and 24 runs at 64²×1, 128²×2 and 256²×3). No frame pays for a whole build. Completed spectra stay in a per-engine cache (12 MiB, least recently used out), so a released, recycled or re-created simulation uploads one instead of drawing it.
    - Storage, cheapest first: the simulation with the same band; an idle one with the same unit spectrum, which takes new uniforms only (Wave Height, Wave Length and Wave Speed edits); one idle for `WATER_FFT_RECYCLE_RUNS` (60); a new lease, up to `WATER_FFT_MAX_SIMULATIONS` (3) per scene. At the cap, or after a ledger refusal, the longest-idle simulation takes the new spectrum (one h0 upload); with none idle, the water stays analytic. So bodies viewed in turn keep their own simulations, and repeated wave edits never pile up storage.
    - It is released after 120 unrequested observer runs, and at once when FFT turns off or FFT Size / FFT Cascades change.
  - Memory: leased under the managed render ledger's `water` category: 96 · cascades · N² bytes (0.4 MiB at 64²×1, 3 MiB at 128²×2, 18 MiB at 256²×3). A refusal keeps analytic-only water and retries after 120 runs.
  - Readiness never blocks: `ready` stays false until the first dispatch lands (and again after a context restore or a new spectrum). Render analytic waves only until then; nothing marks scene readiness.
  - Device: needs float render targets (`textureFloatRender`); otherwise the device clamp turns FFT off and Play readback reports it Clamped.
  - Limits: no mips (Babylon 9.29 WebGPU generates array mips for layer 0 only), so a consumer must fade each cascade by pixel footprint; no foam history; spatial repetition per patch is hidden by the incommensurate patches, the non-periodic analytic swell and chop, and built-in water's per-cascade noise modulation.
  - Engine state: the passes run inside the before-render observer and restore the caller's target, viewport and render state through `engine-drawing-state.ts`. That helper restores Babylon's alpha sentinel −1 (after a cache reset) as ALPHA_DISABLE and only unbinds when no target was bound, so WebGPU opens no empty swapchain pass.
  - Verification: `waterFftSynthesize` mirrors every GPU pass on the CPU; `water-fft-spectrum.test.ts` checks it against directly summed travelling waves. No browser test compares the GPU output.
- FFT detail in built-in water (`WaterMaterialPlugin`, numeric define `SLATE_WATER_FFT` = the cascades sampled, `WATER_FFT_DEFINE`):
  - Compiled with the device-effective FFT Cascades while FFT Ocean Detail is on and Detail Waves is above 0; otherwise 0, which compiles every term out (Low, Medium, Detail Waves 0, the Stylized default, devices without float targets). The define follows quality and the asset only, never readiness: prewarming compiles the variant that runs, and a band that becomes ready or is rebuilt never recompiles water. Until the band is ready (or while it does not match the compiled cascades) the variant binds a one-texel, one-layer placeholder at gain 0 with every cascade faded (`FFT_FADED` as its fade frequency). Neither stage takes a tap, and the surface is exactly the analytic one without the define. So a band still building, refused by the ledger or beyond the per-scene simulation cap costs only the fade arithmetic.
  - Demand: each draw of a variant that samples the band calls `waterFftForSurface` (`hardBindForSubMesh`; the outline mask's `bindVertexWaves` too). The water before-render observer requests the band for visible built-in surfaces (`requestWaterFft` with its active test) and runs `updateSceneWaterFft` once per run. Water whose shader never samples the band never creates one.
  - Binding: one sampler, `slateWaterFftSampler` (`WATER_FFT_SAMPLER`), declared in the fragment and, with the vertex part, in the vertex stage: one WebGL2 texture unit read by both, one WebGPU binding visible to both. Uniforms: `slateWaterFft` = (g = Detail Waves · Wave Scale, 0 until ready; Steepness; metres per pixel per metre of view depth and its constant part, from the pass's projection and target height) and `slateWaterFftCascade0-2` = (1 / patch size, uv offset `fract(origin / patch size) + 0.5 / N` in float64, upper band edge k_hi). k_hi, the cascade's shortest wavelength, is the fade frequency of both stages. Per draw it is allocation-free; band edges are recomputed only when the band's layout changes.
  - Vertex (GPU waves only, `fftVertexSource`, numeric define `SLATE_WATER_FFT_VERTEX`, `WATER_FFT_VERTEX_DEFINE`): each cascade's (Dx, H, Dz) at the vertex's rest point. H is scaled by g, D by g · Steepness and then by the bank fade with the swell's offset. Each cascade fades by the swell's mesh filter on its shortest wavelength: full weight while the larger of the mesh spacing and twice the view footprint is under a quarter of it, none from half of it. The band has no mips, so a vertex point-samples every wavelength of a cascade it keeps. Only cascades the mesh resolves completely displace it; the fragment carries the rest, so nothing aliases into the geometry or crawls along silhouettes, and faded cascades skip their taps. Every cascade's vertex weight is 0 where the mesh spacing is at least half the first cascade's shortest wavelength, so the vertex part compiles only into surfaces whose finest rest-grid spacing (`WaterMaterialPlugin.meshSpacing`, set by the water mesh whenever it builds a grid) is below that: the define is then the band's cascades, otherwise 0, with no vertex changed. High's first cascade (shortest wavelength Wave Length / 23) needs cells under Wave Length / 46, so at the default Surface Resolution High's band stays per-pixel on Global Water and on lakes and oceans; only volumes with cells that fine (a 1 m puddle, or a small volume at a very high Surface Resolution) displace by it. Ultra's narrower first cascade (Wave Length / 9.7) displaces Global Water's dense core at the default Surface Resolution (cells of Wave Length / 36 at Ultra's mesh density). Culling bounds add the band's horizontal bound only where the vertex part is compiled. The horizontal part shrinks where the combined determinant (the swell's shear plus the band's ∂D terms) would drop below `WATER_JACOBIAN_FLOOR`. Height and offset reach `vSlateWater.x` and `vSlateWaterFlow.zw`, so contacts, terrain depth and the fragment's rest point include them.
  - Fragment (both styles, `fftFragmentSource`): each cascade at the fragment's rest point, so the detail rides the waves instead of sliding over them. Each fades by the rest-plane footprint along its longer axis on its highest wavenumber, gone before its shortest waves are under about four and a half pixels long (1 − smoothstep(0.45, 1.4, k·F), `FFT_FRAGMENT_FADE`, as the chop octaves fade), so nothing aliases or shimmers; faded cascades skip their taps. The swell's later curve (0.6 to 2.2) let a cascade's shortest waves reach three pixels, where the sun's lobe and the Fresnel, both far from linear in the normal, aliased them into crawling diagonal hatching across the sun path. Each cascade's strength also drifts with a world noise (the large one for the first, the medium one for the rest, mean 1), so no patch shows as a tile. The first cascade's patch (2 to 4 Wave Lengths, the band's largest) is also sampled a second time at an offset of golden-ratio fractions of the patch and crossfaded with the first sample, variance preserving, by a slow beat of the swell warp's phases (3·K₀ − 2·K₁ + K₂, about two Wave Lengths across) that both stages compute alike, so no patch repeats unchanged (two more fetches, High and Ultra only). The band's slope reaches the shading normal through the combined Jacobian (J⁻ᵀ∇H) while the swell keeps its own. The band's Jacobian joins the analytic one (scaled down below the floor) in the Jacobian foam, so small waves break on steep crests.
  - Faded cascades add no roughness. The chop octaves and capillaries, which cover the same wavelengths, already widen the sun's lobe by the slope they lose; adding the band's lost slope variance as well made distant water hazy in the browser proof, so distant water keeps the look it has without the band.
  - CPU vertex path (Custom Material water's mesh, `setWaterGpuWaves(mesh, false)`): it cannot read the band, so its geometry stays the analytic surface and nothing is displaced twice. Built-in shading on it still adds the fragment detail at its rest point.
  - Not displaced by the band: Babylon's `DepthRenderer` and picking (both see the rest grid, as for the swell), and physics, queries and buoyancy (analytic only, by design). The band is anchored to rest positions, so on rivers it does not drift with the current.
- Chop drifts out of step over time (both styles, every tier): each chop octave is a plane wave, so without help a point sees the same chop again at whole multiples of the octave's period. Each octave's phase also gains `CHOP_SHIFT[i]` (about ±3 rad) times a rotating mix of the centred large and gust noises, cos θ·(large − ½) + sin θ·(gust − ½), with θ = 0.031 × the first octave's angular frequency × time reduced on the CPU (`slateWaterChopShift.xy`, about a 50 s cycle). Patches of sea drift in and out of step with their neighbours over tens of seconds, the crests' speed changing by a few percent, so nothing swims; two MADs per octave, no fetch. Together with the two envelope clocks (above) and the travelling swell warp, a still view's chop decorrelates within a few periods instead of recurring. Each octave's phase is also modulated on two tones (`chopFm`): A·(sin(Ω₁·t + ψ₁) + sin(Ω₂·t + ψ₂)), its depth A between about 0.6 and 2.3 radians across the gust and large noises (tens of metres, so the local wavenumber changes by a few percent at most), Ω₁ a whole combination of the envelope clocks at about a third to a half of the octave's angular frequency and Ω₂ a slow difference of them (4% to 16% of the first octave's), with world phases from the gust and large noises. A plane wave's crests otherwise pass a point at one frequency: a still view of calm water, where the chop carries most of the slope, bobbed at the first octave's period with one dominant line in its temporal spectrum (Painted's calm: about 3,000 times the median bin, frame autocorrelation 0.39 two seconds later). The first tone spreads the octave over sidebands, keeping a Bessel J₀(A)² share in the carrier; the second, in an irrational ratio to it, turns them into a dense comb, so a model of the first octave over 20 s correlates about 0.06 two seconds on instead of 0.5. The crests surge and linger by up to about a third of their speed. Two sines per octave evaluated.
- Aerial perspective (every look, `slateWaterHaze`): `swAir` is the share of the view's light the air has replaced with the horizon's at this eye distance: an exponential that is half at 0.4 of the active camera's far plane, combined with a ramp over 75-97% of it so the far clip never shows as a line (`waterHazeConstants`; the range is capped at 8000 m so a very long far plane still hazes at atmospheric distances; orthographic views have none). Scene fog takes precedence (`#ifdef FOG`): the exponential only acts on what fog leaves, and the ramp stays, its horizon colour being the fog's. Realistic's hazed water is level and sharp, mirrors the horizon band (the lifted reflection ray's knee tightens toward the horizon) and, from Medium, takes the sky's own colour just above the horizon from the scene copy over the last half degree: a horizontal average of the row at the pixel's azimuth (3 taps, 5 from High; taps behind geometry such as a landscape drop out), damped by the fixed-point average so cloud texture never streaks across columns. Painted mixes to the same colour (opaque, foam thinned, refraction off) and keeps its crest glow to a thin band that fades with footprint and distance. Toon steps deep water in four hard bands (about 40, 110, 270 and 600 m for a 2 km far plane) toward the horizon tone, the last all the way.
- Swell footprint fade: a swell component fades by the phase it advances over one sample. The mesh drops it at 1.56 to π rad per vertex spacing (`swvF`); the fragment's height, offset and resolved share fade at 0.6 to 2.2 rad per pixel footprint along the component's heading, and Realistic shades only the slope that stays under 0.25 to 1.0 rad per pixel (`SWELL_SLOPE_FADE`, about 25 down to 6 pixels a cycle); the slope it drops goes to roughness (`swLost`), so a long swell row at a grazing angle never prints a stripe.
- Both styles are world-space: patterns never slide with the camera. Footprint- and derivative-based filtering only fades octaves, foam patterns and sparkles before they would alias. Sun lobes widen by their normal's variation across the pixel, and sun glints and marks are world-anchored, filtered by the pixel footprint and lit over smooth lifetimes rather than per-frame randomness, so every look's sun path shimmers instead of flickering. One GLSL-shaped source is translated to WGSL, so WebGL2 and WebGPU match. Built-in water reflects the visible skybox when no explicit environment cube is assigned; a scene-owned, leased texture view leaves the skybox sampling and custom materials unchanged. Shader world positions add the scene's floating-origin offset (`slateWaterOrigin`), because the app engines use large-world rendering and Babylon then supplies eye-relative positions; without it every pattern would travel with the camera.
- Waves: **Choppiness** (0-1) sharpens swell crests and flattens troughs, **Wave Spread** (0-1) fans the swell components around Wave Direction for a confused sea, and **Crest Foam** breaks the steepest crests into whitecaps. Rendering, queries and buoyancy share the same crest profile (`evaluateWaterWaves`; the allocating `waterWaveProfile` is deprecated). To hide tiling, the wind-chop octaves use non-harmonic scales and headings, a bounded noise warp bends the chop domain (a rotation about the world origin used to compress the chop without limit far from it), each octave's phase drifts with two noise fields so its crests wander, paired octaves fade in opposition so one crossing direction dominates at a time, and a slow world-space gust field varies chop strength and foam across open water. The shared value noises' lattices are turned to different fixed angles (medium 0.29, large 0.53, gust −1.07 radians), so their cells never line up with the world axes or each other into rectangles. The swell itself no longer repeats (its eight components and shared warp, under Wave model). Realistic contact foam fades to nothing away from every object (its patch texture used to pass its cut along the bubble web's brightest borders everywhere, netting the open sea in a faint, regular crackle at Medium and up). Low's Realistic wind streaks break up along the streaky foam texture into scraps instead of running as unbroken white lines. Painted scales its primary swell's height and slope by 1.43 × the root-sum-square of the primary components' amplitudes (their sum, before, read eight shared components as low, flat waves).
- Terrain field: each built-in surface owns a CPU-built **water field** (`WaterField`), an RGBA8 texture over the terrain that meets it (at most 512 by 512 cells, 0.2 m or coarser): R is the signed distance to the terrain shoreline, G the water depth over terrain, B the same depth finely over the shallows (`fineDepthSpan` from just below the lowest trough: every displaced waterline plus 4 m, at least `WATER_FIELD_FINE_DEPTH_SPAN` = 5 m, about 2 cm per step for default waves), A 255 over real terrain. Past an underwater landscape edge the cells carry the edge's depth (each ring the mean of the previous one) and A falls from 240 to 0 over `WATER_FIELD_EDGE_RAMP` (16 m; unbounded water's field extends that far past the terrain), so the shoreline slope finds no false cliff at the coverage edge (an unknown cell's sentinel used to draw a shore line and a calm band along it, the "diagonal line" and "inner rectangle") and terrain depth hands over to the shelving estimate gradually. Only underwater terrain seeds that extension, and only for the ramp's rings: past a dry edge (a coast tile, a valley lake's rim) there is no floor under the water, so those cells stay unknown and keep the shore distance measured from the real land. Only real terrain (A above `WATER_FIELD_TERRAIN_ALPHA`, 0.97) cuts water or measures the shoreline from depth over slope; extended cells keep the stored shore distance, so neither a transparent gap (Realistic) nor a foam band (Stylized) appears past an edge above or just under the water. Shallows read B, so gentle shores shade and foam without depth terraces. The field hands over to the open-water defaults across its last three texels instead of switching off at its bounds, so its edge never draws a straight seam through the swash and shallows. A bounded body's field extends beyond its bounds by more than those three texels, so the body's own rim (a river's mouth over a beach) never lies in the handover, where terrain depth fades out and the rim drew as an opaque pale strip above the sand; Realistic no longer adds a second edge fade of its own. Open-water edges: where a terrain field is bound but holds no terrain under a finite body's bank (an ocean's outer edge, a lake reaching past a landscape's border), that bank is no shore: no shore foam or swash, and the water fades out over 6 m instead (`OPEN_EDGE_FADE`). Realistic and Painted treat a bank that ends over water-covered terrain, metres short of the terrain's own shoreline (a lake drawn smaller than its basin, a river narrower than its channel), the same way over 2.5 m (`OPEN_EDGE_FADE_TERRAIN`), so it never stands as a cut sheet with a bright rim above the bed; Toon keeps its drawn rim there. Realistic water also thins out over its last quarter metre of terrain depth, so a bank never shows a hard lip. It refreshes when a landscape or the surface moves. While its inputs change every frame (a tide, a carried or animated body, a landscape being sculpted) it refills at most every 150 ms (`WATER_FIELD_MOVE_MS`), keeping the previous texture in between, and once more after they settle, so the final pose always lands; a single change after a quiet spell, and a first fill, refill at once. Rest heights are measured every fourth cell and interpolated where all four samples hold water; cells whose samples straddle the water's edge are measured exactly, so the shoreline does not move and only the edge pays a full rest-surface evaluation. Its per-frame change check compares numbers only (each enabled landscape's data identity and world matrix, the surface matrix, the wave envelope and a finite body's bounds) over the scene's landscape roots (`sceneLandscapeRoots`, kept by landscape creation and scene membership), so unchanged frames neither scan `scene.meshes` nor allocate. Where a landscape overlaps the surface it supplies true terrain depth for transmittance and shore foam; elsewhere depth is estimated from bank distance, a shelving floor and component Depth. It is not scene-depth refraction.
- Contact field: objects meeting the water live in a separate, objects-only **contact field** (`WaterContactField`, sampler `slateWaterContactSampler`, one more material sampler within the reserved eight). Channel *k* stores the signed horizontal distance to object cross-sections at the local rest height + {−1, −⅓, ⅓, 1} × the wave envelope (`waterWaveEnvelope` + 0.05 m), negative inside closed objects and unsigned for open meshes. Surfaces just above or below a layer add a 1.5×-weighted vertical gap, so shallow hulls, rafts and deck undersides between layers still meet the water. It has its own bounds around the objects and cells of Contact Foam Width × 3 / 24 (0.06–0.25 m), at most the project's Water **Contact Resolution** per side (256–1024, part of the field's surface state, so a change rebuilds it), doubling for widely spread objects. Distances are clamped to three Contact Foam Widths (1–8 m). On rivers and tilted volumes each part of an object is cut relative to the rest height beneath it (interpolated from about one rest sample per metre), so a long log or hull meets the sloping water at its own waterline along its length.
- Contacts follow the actual waterline: the shader blends the two layers around each fragment's rendered wave height (`vSlateWater.x`), so foam moves up and down sloped hulls, rocks and cones as waves pass, with no CPU rebake while waves animate or when paused. The field rebuilds only when objects appear, disappear, move or deform, or when the surface transform, wave envelope, Contact Foam Width or Contact Resolution change. Scenes are scanned every 100 ms; tracked objects are checked each frame, and a moving object recomputes and uploads only its neighbourhood, at most every 33 ms. A moving surface (its transform or rest heights) re-cuts every contact at most every 150 ms and once more when it settles, rather than every frame.
  - Many surfaces stay cheap. One list of contact-eligible scene meshes is gathered and shared by every surface's scan (re-gathered after 50 ms, when the scene's mesh count changes, or for a surface's full rescan), and surfaces' periodic scans fall in six phases of the 100 ms period, so they never all scan in one frame.
  - Moving-object rebuilds share a per-scene budget of 2 ms a frame (`WATER_CONTACT_FRAME_BUDGET_MS`): one always runs, and once the frame has spent the budget, further surfaces wait for a later frame. The surface that waited longest last frame goes first in the next, whatever order the surfaces are visited in, so the first surfaces never starve the later ones; and none waits past 100 ms or two frames, whichever is longer (at 15-20 fps a waiting surface's contacts are at most two frames stale). Full rebuilds (a new or reshaped surface) never wait but count against the frame's budget. With one moving object nothing changes; with many, contacts update at 10 Hz or more instead of every 33 ms.
  - The distance transform (`distanceTransform`) runs without closures or allocation, skips lines without any seed, and for a rebuild finishes only the rows and columns it reads (`DistanceRegion`), skipping rows whose every distance is beyond the contact range. Its output inside the range is identical. Meshes whose vertices are rewritten in place under a fixed transform (for example a simulated Cable) re-slice when their geometry's position revision changes (`watchGeometry`, a shared `onGeometryUpdated` counter also used by the shadow map refresh), with the same throttle. Thin-instanced meshes reuse the previous scan's placements until their transform, geometry, instance count or instance matrices change.
- Contact candidates are visible world meshes, instances and every thin instance, including objects whose centre stands on land beside a finite body. Editor and debug helpers (the shared `isEditorHelperMesh`: grid, camera bounds, navmesh debug overlay, pick proxies, actor origins, billboards, volumes, collider visuals, Play helpers and debug overlays; tag new helpers with `markEditorHelperMesh`), lines, skyboxes and other infinite-distance meshes (a sky dome follows the camera, so where its geometry crosses the surface is no waterline; one used to draw a foam ring at the horizon), landscapes, water and removal volumes are ignored. Skinned, morphed and material-deformed meshes use their undeformed geometry.
- Small waves: near contacts the normals carry outward-travelling ripples (moving at the deep-water speed of their wavelength, fading over about 1.4 Contact Foam Widths, bent and broken by drifting noise, faded by `fwidth` before they alias) plus extra wind chop. Realistic water shows a dense churned band at the waterline that breaks within about half a Contact Foam Width into sparse, translucent patches, with a few flecks carried out on ripple crests; the band stretches downstream (along the current where the water flows, otherwise downwind) on every tier, and from High a trailing wake follows each object: two more contact-field taps upstream (at 0.4 and 0.8 of the contact range, explicit level, same sampler) find the object, and the plume widens with distance at about the Kelvin angle and thins as it trails; Stylized draws a solid collar on the waterline with a looser, blob-broken band the ripples push outward, chunky foam arcs on the ripple crests from Medium, and shades the ripples as soft rings.
- Surf zone and soft waterline (render only, every look, ALU): where the rest depth is under about 1.3 to 3.4 Wave Heights (`SURF_DEPTH`; a gentle beach breaks over a wide band, a steep one a narrow one) the steepest swell slot's phase puts breaking patches on each crest, trailing seaward (`SURF_WAKE`) and broken along the crest by a noise per wave, so foam advances shoreward with the crests, and streaks stretched along the seaward direction (the terrain's gradient where measured, else the bank's) trail out from the swash (`swSurfBreak`, joining each look's foam density; Toon thresholds it and adds two scalloped lines stepping seaward with the waves). The run-up reaches up to 10 m on gentle slopes and thins over its last 22% instead of 12%. Over known terrain the water's coverage fades over the last 0.4 m of depth (`SOFT_SHORE_DEPTH`) in Realistic and Painted, shrinking to a few centimetres where the terrain slope is steep (walls and cliffs stay crisp); Toon keeps its drawn edge.
- Shore swash (render only, every look and tier, ALU; no texture fetch beyond the terrain and contact fields): one swash cycle per two waves of the steepest swell slot (ω and k at half the slot's; one per wave ran up and drained in about a second for default waves, a flicker rather than a swash). The CPU passes `slateWaterSwash` (`waterSwashConstants`): the clock θ = ω·t − ½·k·(d·origin) reduced to [0, 2π) in float64 with the whole cycles it removed (mod 4096, as Toon's crest count), the bores' shoreward wavenumber κ (3·k, about the speed of a bore in half a metre of water, but bores at least 2.5 Foam Widths apart and never below ¾·k, so they travel shoreward on every side of an island), and the swash's vertical excursion D (1.1 × Wave Height × Wave Scale + 0.18 m, 0 for flat water). The shader adds −½·k·(d·rest) and the large noise, so the cycle rolls along a coast as the swell's crests do; each wave's reach is a hash of its world cycle index times the slot's wave group there (sets of larger waves), so it never changes as the clock wraps. A bore runs up in the first 30% of a cycle and drains for the rest. Over known terrain the backwash uncovers the shallows down to D of water (at most D over the terrain slope, and 6 m, from the waterline), the water thinning out over the last eighth of that excursion; the wet film's age is the time since the receding edge passed (this cycle's, else the last one's). Bores travel through the surf (about 2.5 swash widths plus four Foam Widths) toward the shore and arrive as the next run-up, each as strong as the run-up it brings; their foam is sized in metres (a band about a Foam Width behind each front), since bores are tens of metres apart for long swell. Fragments beyond the surf skip the whole block (a branch with no derivatives), and so do fragments over no known terrain unless the water there is shallow enough for the swell to break (rest depth under about three Wave Heights, the terrain's where known, else a shelving bank's estimate): small ponds and narrow rivers, whose banks are all within the surf width, no longer pay for it on almost every fragment. Bores break only where the water is shallow for the swell (rest depth under about two and a half Wave Heights) and are thinned in wide patches by the medium noise, so calm lakes and steep banks get no rings standing off the shore and no coast reads as one continuous ring; coasts facing the steepest slot's travel take the full bores, lee coasts (which the swell reaches only by wrapping round) a quarter, so the fronts arrive from the windward side instead of ringing an island evenly (the terrain's seaward slope direction from the shoreline taps, one `normalize`). Physics, queries and the vertex swell are untouched.
- Contact swell (render only, every look and tier, ALU): near contacts the steepest slot's phase at the nearest hull point drives the contact foam: it pushes out and thickens as the water climbs the hull, peaks with the crest and, as the water drops, a wider, thinner ring clings briefly before it drains; a calm surface keeps a steady collar. Ripples go out in one burst per rise at their group speed (`slateWaterContactInfo.w` carries ω over it), and from Medium (Realistic and Toon; Painted on every tier) foam is shed downstream in puffs that drift with the wake; Realistic's fade within about a Contact Foam Width of the object, so they never scatter over open water. Open water skips it (a branch).
- Contact foam has its own strength: it stays clearly visible at a low Foam Amount (at least 0.85 once Foam Amount exceeds about 0.1). Foam Amount 0 or Contact Foam Width 0 removes it. **Contact Foam Width** (metres) sets its width and the ripple wavelength. In Realistic water only the dense collar right at the object is solid: away from it the foam thins, its edge softens over a couple of pixels and it opens into the bubble web's holes (Low: its band breaks along its lace), so wide contact foam never reads as a flat cut-out.
- Four layers approximate objects whose cross-section changes quickly with height; horizontal faces between layers get approximate foam. A piece thinner than the layer spacing (a raft, a plank or a float riding the waves between two layers without crossing either) is also cut at its own mid-height, and that closed section stands in for every layer within one spacing of it that it does not cross, so its hull is the waterline (it used to read as a third of a metre away and showed no contact foam at all). A static thin object within one spacing of the waterline therefore meets crests up to one spacing early. Very widely spread objects share one field and so get coarser cells.
- Where several objects' distances meet, a hard minimum creases along their bisector, and every contour drawn from it (Toon's rings and lines) kinked there into straight cracks with right-angle corners. Each layer's signed distance is rounded like a smooth minimum where everything under the kernel is far from any object: two box passes of radius about half a Contact Foam Width (`smoothContacts`), blended in only where the smallest exact distance under the kernel (`minimumFilter`) is three radii or more, so each hull's waterline keeps its exact place.
- Placing a surface each frame allocates nothing unless its grid is rebuilt: each enabled surface advances its shader clock and compares its world matrix and grid inputs numerically.
  - Per-vertex rest data (spacing, normals, current, bank distance, depth) depend only on the grid and the volume's rotation and scale. Only a rebuilt grid or a rotated or scaled volume recomputes and uploads them.
  - A translated volume uploads nothing on the GPU path: the shader reads world positions, and Babylon moves the local bounds with the world matrix. The CPU path re-derives its world rest points for its next animation step.
  - Global Water's grid lines are fixed offsets from its snapped centre, and open water's rest data are uniform. A centre that moved by whole cells (walking, sailing or orbiting cameras) therefore only adds the new centre to the CPU rest points and extents. On the GPU-wave path the uploaded positions are the grid lines relative to the centre and the vertex shader adds the centre back (`slateWaterGridOffset`, bound per draw from the surface's mesh as the linear part of its world matrix applied to the local centre, to the material and to the shared outline mask's vertex pass alike, before the rest point the swell reads), so a recentre uploads no vertex data, allocates nothing and creates no GPU buffers. CPU picks (`scene.pick`, `pickWithRay`) keep hitting the water where it is: the mesh's `intersects` tests its bounds as given, then its triangles with the ray moved back by the offset, and moves the picked point forward. The CPU path (Custom Material water, or GPU waves switched off) uploads absolute positions and a zero offset.
  - Only a changed extent (camera far plane), rotation, scale, body, grid step (Wave Length and Surface Resolution) or Mesh Density builds a new grid. On the CPU path, repeated simulation times reuse the vertex buffers unless the definition changes (see live definition edits); advancing time still updates custom Water Surface data.
- Surfaces that no pass drew last frame and that lie outside every active camera's frustum (this frame's matrices; a scene without a camera counts as seeing them) skip their CPU vertex work and their terrain and contact refreshes until they are seen again. Their clock, grid and bounds stay current.
- Each frame a surface's world matrix is recomputed only when it is out of sync with its transform or parent (`isSynchronized`), not forced.
- A surface that stays disabled for 2 s (`WATER_RELEASE_DISABLED_MS`) releases its terrain and contact textures but keeps their CPU data; enabling it re-uploads them after its placement (and refills or re-cuts them only if something they read changed meanwhile), at most two surfaces a frame (`WATER_RESTORES_PER_FRAME`), so a section that enables many bodies at once spreads the uploads; the others draw without the textures until their turn. A disabled surface otherwise costs one enabled check a frame.
- Water meshes never cast shadows, even when the actor's participation casts (`neverCastsShadows`); they still receive them. As casters their dynamic geometry would refresh every local shadow map each frame and split large grids into extra draws.
- Built-in water lazily shares its scene light estimate and enabled removal-volume candidates within each scene frame/render pass. Each material applies its own environment strength and retains its surface's nearest four cutters across repeated submesh binds. The next frame or camera/render-target pass refreshes lights, volume membership and transforms; updates should settle before drawing that pass.
- Terrain stays cached at rest height: the shader adds rendered wave displacement for depth and cut-outs, and divides the displaced depth by the terrain slope for a local shoreline distance. The slope comes from central differences of the fine depth one cell apart (`slateWaterFieldStep`, whose z carries the fine span), which stay continuous where a bilinear field's own gradient would step. Where a tap is clamped beyond the fine range (a floor deeper than any trough reaches, or land above the highest crest) the waves cannot move the shoreline, so the stored rest distance (R) stands: steep coasts and storm waves keep their shore foam, Stylized outlines and near-shore calming, and the full-range depth's 8-bit steps (about 16 cm) never read as slopes. Measured from those steps, a gentle floor a few metres deep drew contour rings of shore foam far from any shore. The depth encoding expands for high wave envelopes.
- Cut-outs: landscapes remove every water surface wherever terrain rises above the water, so a Global Water Volume stops at an island's shoreline instead of drawing through it. **Water Removal Volume** (`WaterRemovalVolumeComponent`) removes water inside a Box, Sphere, Cylinder or Capsule (Width, Height, Length in metres; Width is the diameter for round shapes, Length applies to boxes, and the actor transform positions, rotates and scales it). It cuts every water component, including a boat hull's interior or a well. The editor shows it as a blue wireframe; it is invisible in Play. Up to four removal volumes nearest each surface are cut in the shader; queries and buoyancy honour every enabled volume and landscape, returning Found false inside a cut. A Custom Material draws the full surface; queries remain cut.

### Water blending

Water bodies within the project's **Water Blend Distance** of each other merge into one surface, so a river runs into a lake and the lake opens into an ocean without seams. The setting lives in Project Settings > Rendering > Water (`RenderProjectSettings.water.blendDistance`, metres, default 8, 0 turns blending off). No quality tier changes it (`PROJECT_RENDER_SETTING_OWNERS`): rendering and worker water queries read the same value.

- Shared kernel (`@babylonslate/core` `water-blend.ts`): `evaluateWaterBlend` gives the blended surface at a rest point as one body sees it. Every body whose rest surface comes within the distance R (and within the vertical tolerance V = max(0.25 m, R/4) of this body's rest height) takes part:
  - Coverage φ = smoothstep(−R/2, R/2, bank distance) times a vertical fade (full below V, none at 2V). A body therefore reaches R/2 past its footprint and blends over R across its edge.
  - Shares: each φ times (1 − φ) of every participant of a higher class, normalised to 1. Classes: Global Water Volume < Ocean < Lake < River < Puddle. Inside a higher body the lower one gives way. A river keeps its current into a lake until its end, and a lake keeps its own water inside an ocean. A reach narrower than the distance always shares a little with the body around it.
  - Union shoreline: the smooth maximum (radius 2R) of the bank distances, in ascending body order so every participant agrees. A body leaving the blend (vertically, or over the outer half of its reach) fades out of it, so the shoreline never steps where it stops taking part. Edges that face another body are no shore: no shore foam, edge fade or bank fade there. Gaps narrower than R fill with water (terrain above the water still cuts it away).
  - Rest height, current and Depth average by share.
  - The swell averages Wave Scale and the Gerstner factor (q·s) over participants with the same swell (`sameWaterWaves`), relative to the body's own constants (`heightScale`, `offsetScale`). Where bodies with different swells meet, both calm toward the seam (smoothstep(0.5, 1, same-swell share)), so they meet flat and continuous rather than mismatched.
  - Colours mix toward bodies coloured differently (`foreign` share, the strongest `partner`).
  - Ownership: `margin` is this body's share less the largest other. The body with the largest share draws and answers queries; ties go to the higher class, then the lower index.
- Neighbours (`WaterBlendIndex`): rediscovered only when a body, its pose or revision, or the distance changes. Its `update` compares those inputs and returns at once otherwise. Discovery sorts the bodies' world bounds along X and sweeps; open water pairs with every body at its level. When only poses moved (a body dragged, animated or carried by a platform every tick), only the moved bodies are prepared again, the neighbour lists are rediscovered into reused arrays, and the per-neighbour swell and colour matches are recomputed only where a list changed, so the worker's index allocates nothing beyond the moved bodies' own inputs.
- Queries (`sampleWaterBlend`, the worker's `WaterWorld`): a body with no neighbours, or a point beyond every neighbour's reach plus the horizontal wave envelope, takes the unblended `sampleWaterSurface` path unchanged. Otherwise:
  - World X/Z inverts under the blended Gerstner offset, whose gain is the union shoreline's bank fade times `offsetScale`. The kernel does not run at every inversion step: it runs at the query point (the gain held constant gives a first rest point), then on a five-point stencil (centre and ±5 cm along X and Z) around that rest point, and the inversion solves against the gain's plane through the stencil. A rest point more than 2 mm from its stencil takes a fresh one (at most twice), and the kernel is read once more at a rest point more than 0.1 mm from its stencil's centre.
